under the title “Studies and Notes in Philology and Literature,” 
appeared in 1907. The continuation of the studies now, after 
an interval of twenty-three years, has been made possible by 
the use of a portion of the gift of the General Education 
Board to Harvard University for the promotion of research

THE DON CARLOS THEME 


By FREDERICK W. C. LIEDER 


ON CARLOS, son of Philip II of Spain by the latter’s first wife, 
Mary of Portugal, was born in 1545 and died at the age of twenty- 
three in 1568. Mystery has always surrounded the life of this unfortu- 
nate prince. We have, on the one hand, facts that are now generally 
accepted by historians — that Carlos was sickly and ugly in appear- 
_ ance, that he showed signs of mental weakness, that this weakness re- 
sulted in the wildest and most irresponsible acts, and that he died as a 
result of his excesses. On the other hand we have literary treatments 
of facts and events supposedly connected with the life of Carlos — that 
he was a romantic and cosmopolitan dreamer who hoped to bring about 
a golden age in Spain and in the rest of the world, that he was in love 
with his stepmother (formerly his fiancée), and that he was condemned 
to death by his own father. 

The story about the rivalry of Philip and Carlos for the love of Eliza- 
beth of Valois and the story of Philip’s inhuman treatment of his son 
seemed to appeal to popular imagination more than did historical facts. 
It is true that Philip, upon the death of his second wife, Mary Tudor of 
England, married Elizabeth of Valois; it is possible that Carlos was at 
one time mentioned as a desirable husband for Elizabeth, but no en- 
gagement was ever officially announced. The story that Philip dis- 
placed his son, that Carlos and Elizabeth continued to love one an- 
other, and that the king was finally responsible for the death of his son 
was given wide currency through the novelette published by St. Réal 
in 1672 (no. 7 in the list of treatments that follow). The influence 
exerted by St. Réal’s story can hardly be overestimated. 

Although important literary treatments of the Don Carlos matter — 
especially those by the Englishman Otway (no. 10), the Frenchman 
Campistron (no. 13), the Italian Alfieri (no. 22), and the German 
Schiller (no. 27) — have led to comparisons and discussions as to their 
deviations from history, only within recent years have attempts been 
made to trace more systematically and thoroughly the spreading of the 
Carlos legend in various literatures and literary forms. It is my plan 
to give a review of the whole subject in the pages that follow. 


4 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


T shall begin by giving the titles of those studies in which ten or more 
treatments of the theme are listed: 


Amédée de la Rousseliére, Don Carlos, Tragédie en cinque actes et en vers 
imitée de Schiller, Liége, 1855. (Rousseliére, in his Introduction, ex- 
presses admiration for Schiller, Otway, and Alfieri, and then gives the 
names of eleven authors to whom he owed nothing. Fourteen treatments 
mentioned.) 

Karl Goedeke, Grundriss der Geschichte der deutschen Dichtung, Dresden, 
1895. (In vol. V, Pt. 2, Goedeke mentions, by title only, seventeen treat- 


ments exclusive of Schiller’s.) 
F. W. C. Lieder, The Don Carlos Theme in Literature. Published in The 


Journal of English and Germanic Philology, vol. IX (October, 1910), no. 4. 
(Thirty-seven treatments, exclusive of translations.) 

F. W. C. Lieder, Schiller’s Don Carlos, Infant von Spanien. Edited with in- 
troduction, bibliography, appendices, notes, and index. New York, 1912. 
(Forty-three treatments, exclusive of translations.) 

Ezio Levi, Storia Poetica di Don Carlos, Pavia, 1914. (About thirty treat- 


ments.) 
A. P. Happel, The Life and Works of the Abbé de Saint-Réal. An unpublished 


doctoral thesis deposited in the Harvard University Library in 1916. 
(Happel mentions fifteen treatments that were apparently influenced by 


St. Réal.) 

Gustave Dulong, L’ Abbé de Saint-Réal, Paris, 1921. (Referred to, in the fol- 
lowing pages, as Dulong, vol. I.) 

Gustave Dulong, Notes et Documents relatifs d ? Abbé de Saint-Réa], Paris, 
1921. (Referred to as Dulong, vol. II. In this volume the author dis- 
cusses in detail twelve dramatic treatments that were influenced by St. 
Réal, and refers casually to others. About twenty-five treatments are 


mentioned in all.) 
Ezio Levi, Il Principe Don Carlos nella Leggenda e nella Poesia, Rome, 1924. 
(This volume is practically a reprint of the work published by Levi in 


1914.) 


Levi, when he published his book in 1914, was unacquainted with the 
titles that precede his in the above list; Dulong, when he brought out 
his thesis in 1921, knew nothing about Levi or about the preceding 
titles; Levi, when he published his reprint in 1924, mentioned the title 
of Dulong’s book in a few footnotes, but he made no use of Dulong’s 
material. 

It will not be my purpose to give long analyses and criticisms of the 
outstanding treatments; this has been done adequately in biographies 
of the authors and in editions of their works. I shall aim, rather, to 
give as complete a list as possible of the treatments of the theme, and 
to state in connection with the less well-known treatments such facts 


The Don Carlos Theme 5 


as may seem pertinent. The treatments are arranged in chronological 
order. : 


1. DIOGENES, ou du moyen d’establir aprés tant de miséres et calamitez 
une bonne et asseurée paix en France, et la rendre plus florissante 
qu'elle ne fust jamais, Liege, 1581. 

For more than ten years after the death of Don Carlos and Elizabeth 

of Valois (1568), accounts of their fate (written, to be sure, mainly by 

Spaniards) had, on the whole, given an extremely favorable picture of 

Philip II. Gradually, however, disquieting rumors began to circulate. 

In June, 1580, Philip issued his ban against William of Orange, staunch 

defender of the rebellious provinces in the Netherlands. In 1581 Wil- 

liam replied with his famous Apologia, in which he boldly accused 

Philip of having poisoned his son Carlos and his wife Elizabeth. It is to 

be noted, however, that William did not accuse Philip of having robbed 

his son of a bride. 

The year 1581 also saw the publication of the curious poem entitled 
Diogenes. The writer appeals to Henry III of France to support the 
King of Portugal and the people of Flanders against the tyranny of 
Philip II. Philip is charged with believing the unjust accusations of 
Ruy Gdémez against Don Carlos and Elizabeth — namely, that they 
loved one another and that their relations were improper. Philip is 
furthermore branded as the man responsible for the deaths of Carlos 
and Elizabeth. 

The poem, though of little historical value, may be regarded as the 
first literary treatment in which the love of Carlos for his stepmother 
is brought out. This all-important element in the story was strongly 
emphasized by St. Réal, whose Don Carlos: Nouvelle Historique (no. 7) 
so many later writers utilized as a source. St. Réal states in one of his 
prefaces that the poem Diogenes “‘traite cette matiére 4 fond.” 

The poem (written in Alexandrines) was reprinted by Anatole de 
Montaiglon in Recueil de Poésies Frangoises des XVe et XVIe Siécles 
morales, facétieuses, historiques, Bibliothéque Elzevirienne, vol. IX 
[Paris, 1865]. Montaiglon’s description of the poem is thoroughgoing: 


Cette piéce, que nous ne nous rappelons pas avoir vu signaler, et qui est 
digne d’étre connue 4 plus d’un titre, se trouve a la bibliothéque de |’Arsenal. 
C’est un in-16 de 14 feuillets non chiffrés, sous les signatures A-D, et de 32 
lignes 4 la page. . . . Il y a pourtant lieu de croire que notre auteur anonyme 
étoit protestant. Les expressions qu’il emploie, pages 12 et 13, Sa connois- 
sance des affaires de la Floride, conquise par une expédition protestante, 


6 Frederick W.C. Lieder 


paroissent des raisons suffisantes pour le supposer. En méme temps ce n’est 
pas un Francois, mais un Flamand, plus écrivain que bien des auteurs fran- 
cais ses contemporains, mais qui porte la marque native de |’étranger dans 
certaines constructions obscures, dans des sens singuliers, dans des mots peu 
usités, et dans certaines duretés, sentant moins la personnalité que le manque 
d’expérience et une habitude vicieuse, toutes choses qu’il n’auroit pas s’il 
avoit vécu dans le vrai milieu de la langue. La piéce, au reste, n’en a que plus 
de mérite et d’intérét, et sa haine de Philippe II et de |’Espagnol en est 
d’autant plus vigoureuse et légitime. C’est elle qui l’inspire tout entiére, et 
c’est le sentiment dont on va trouver ici le développement et |’expression. 


2. Lr PATISSIER DE MADRIGAL EN ESpAGNE, EsTIME EstrE Dom 
Car os, Fits pu Roy PuILirPe, Poitiers, 1596. 

Another edition (perhaps the first) appeared in Paris in the same year 

(1596). ; 

The story (there is a copy of the Poitiers edition in the British Mu- 
seum) covers only fourteen pages. Philip had commanded the Prince 
of Eboli (called Roderic de Gommes de Sylva Portugais), the Count of 
Chinchon, and two others to put Carlos to death. These four men de- 
cided to let Carlos escape on condition that he change his name and 
keep himself out of sight during the lifetime of the king. There ap- 
peared years later, at Madrigal, a man with a black beard tinged with 
gray. He prepared, with a few assistants, ‘‘certaines pAtisseries et 
semblables delicatesses,” and sold them to those who cared to buy. 
Suspicion and doubt caused his arrest. He suggested an appeal to the 
king, and after advice had come from Madrid, was guarded in a 
chateau called ‘La Motte de Medine,” where he had kindly treat- 
ment, but was not allowed to talk to anyone except his guards. The 
story states that the ‘‘patissier” had appeared eighteen months before 
(that is, in 1594 or 1595), and that he seemed to be about forty-four 
years old. This would suggest about 1550 as the date of Carlos’s birth; 
as a matter of fact, he was born in 1545. 

E. Fournier (Variétés historiques et littéraires, II [Paris, 1855], 27), 
quotes in a footnote a certain M. Leber: ‘‘M. Leber voit dans ce livret 
une anecdote singuliére d’ou il résulteroit, dit-il, que D. Carlos auroit 
vécu longtemps aprés l’époque ot l’on suppose que son pére le fit as- 
sasiner. . . . El prouve au moins, ajoute-t-il, que le sort de ce prince fut 
toujours un probléme, méme du temps de Philippe II, qui ne mourut 
qu’en 1598.” 


3. PIERRE DE BOURDEILLE, ABBE DE BRANTOME, Vies des Grands 
Capitains (especially the articles Philippe II, Roy d’Espagne and 


The Don Carlos Theme 7 


Don Carlos), also Vies des Dames Illustres (especially the article 
Elizabeth de France, Reyne d’ Espagne). Written before 1614. First 
printed in an edition of Brantéme’s works in nine volumes, Leyden, 
1665-66. 


Brantéme’s dates were 1540 to 1614. Though he had spent some 
time at the Court of Spain in November, 1564, and had seen the char- 
acters whom he describes, his work is hardly a historical account, for 
he repeats the rumors that were current in France. According to 
Brantéme, Carlos was condemned to death by his father, and was 
strangled; and Carlos, moreover, had gone through the trying experi- 
ence of losing his fiancée when his father decided to marry her. St. 
Réal, who mentions Brantéme as one of his sources, made much of this 
episode, which, in turn, had a powerful effect on writers who subse- 
quently treated the Don Carlos theme. 


4. Don DiI£cGo XIMENEZ DE Encis0, El Principe Don Carlos. Written, 
and probably performed, between 1619 and 1629. 


Much confusion has prevailed about this play. It was printed for 
the first time in volume XXVIII of Comedias de Varios Autores, En 
Huesca, 1634; the author’s name is spelled “‘Anciso,” and the play is 
described on the title-page as “Comedia Famosa.” Enciso based his 
play on Cabrera’s Historia de Felipe Segundo, which appeared in 1619. 
In some editions of Spanish plays El Principe Don Carlos is attributed 
to Lope de Vega, in others to Montalvan. Ticknor (History of Spanish 
Literature, II [Boston, 1863], 319) mentions Montalvan as the author, 
and this error appears in the catalogue of the Ticknor Collection in the 
Boston Public Library. Dulong (II, 75) makes the same mistake that 
Ticknor made. 

Enciso’s treatment is the first drama dealing with the theme; it is, 
moreover, the most significant of those few treatments that follow his- 
torical facts. Carlos is not in love with his stepmother; he is represented 
as a high-strung, impetuous, feverishly impatient youth, given to wild 
excesses and imbued with the fixed idea that the courtiers are plotting 
to keep him from winning his father’s favor and from sharing in the 
government of his father’s realms, especially Flanders. At the end of 
the play Carlos is miraculously cured from a fever and from injuries 
sustained through a fall. In the version by Cafiizares (no. 19) and the 
stage adaptation by Schaeffer (no. 95), Carlos dies as a result of his 
excesses. 


8 Frederick W.C. Lieder 


A good summary of Enciso’s play may be found in Levi’s works 
(chapter 4 in the editions of 1914 and of 1924). What impresses one 
most is Enciso’s keen sense for historical truth. This is brought out by 
two German historians of Spanish literature, Adolph Fr. von Schack 
and Adolf Schaeffer. Schack says (Geschichte der dramatischen Lit- 
eratur) und Kunst in spanien, II [Berlin, 1845], 536): 


Der Prinz erscheint — sehr verschieden von dem D. Carlos, den die 
triumende Phantasie der neueren Zeit erschaffen hat, aber gewiss in mehr 
Ubereinstimmung mit den historischen Zeugnissen — als ein launenhafter 
und iibermiithiger Wiistling, als ein Tyrann aller seiner Untergebenen, dessen 
- Tod vor dem Regierungsantritt ein wahres Gliick fiir Spanien sei. In der 
Darstellung seines ausgelassenen Treibens sind viele einzelne, offenbar durch 
Tradition tiberlieferte, Anekdoten und Ziige aus seinem Leben benutzt, aus 
denen der Geschichtsschreiber ein neues und interessantes Licht tiber letz- 
teres verbreiten kénnte. 


And Schaeffer (Geschichte des spanischen Nationaldramas, I (Leipzig, 
1890], 400): 


Die Characterzeichnung in diesem Schauspiel ist geradezu uniibertrefflich. 
Philipp IT in seiner ernsten, oft pedantischen Weise; der Prinz in seiner 
riicksichtslosen, aufbrausenden, tyrannischen Art; Herzog Alba von eiserner 
Kaltbliitigkeit und der kaum respectvollen Haltung einer langjahrigen, 
unentbehrlichen Ministers; der Cardinal Espinosa, trotz seiner hohen Stel- 
lung von h6fischerm, furchtsamerm Character: alle diese Figuren machen den 
Eindruck hoher historischer Wahrheit. Wie sehr hat dagegen unser Schiller 
in seinem Don Carlos gegen die Geschichte gefrevelt, wenn auch sein Drama 
demjenigen des Enciso als Dichtung bedeutend iiberlegen ist! 


5. PEDRO CALDERON DE LA Barca, La Vida es Suefio. Written about 
1630. 

It has been held (not without some justification) that Calderén, in 

writing this, the most famous of his plays, was influenced by Enciso’s 

El Principe Don Carlos (no. 4). The case has been stated most clearly 

by Ezio Levi, La Leggenda di Don Carlos nel Teatro Spagnuolo del Sei- 

cento (Revista d’ Italia [1913], pp. 910-911): 

La pit celebre delle rappresentazioni teatrali ispirate dalla leggenda di 
Don Carlos e dal dramma di Enciso é La vita é un sogno di Calderén de la 
Barca. . . . In questa rappresentazioni del triste destino del Principe e della 
natura selvaggia e truce di lui, Calderén deve aver avuto naturalmente 
davante agli occhi il profilo leggendario di Don Carlos. . . . I due drammi 
furono composti nel medesimo tempo: il Don Carlos tra il 1620 e il 1628 e 


La mia é un sogno verso 1630. Quale dunque dei due sara l’originale? La 
risposta non puo essere dubbia: le scene potenti e suggestive di Enciso devono 


The Don Carlos Theme 9 


avere accesa la fantasia di Calderén, come mossero quella di Montalvan, di 
Cafiizares e di tanti altri scrittori drammatici. Gli episodi comuni alle due 
tragedie sono nella Vita é un sogno puramente accessori e descrittivi, mentre 
nel Don Carlos sono cosi connessi con tutta l’ azione che non si po credere 
che essi siano stati creati per altro impulso fuor che quello della stessa neces- 
sita drammatica e dallo svolgimento organico e naturale dell’ argomento. 


The main plot of Calderén’s drama may be briefly outlined. Segis- 
mundo, son of King Basilio of Poland, has been kept in seclusion since 
early infancy — the result of a horoscope cast by the father at the birth 
of the child. The father feared that the son might bring disaster upon 
the kingdom. In time, however, the father relents, decides to have his 
son brought to the palace while in a trance, gives orders that Segis- 
mundo be greeted as King, and proclaims that the son’s fate will be 
determined by his actions. The experiment is a dismal failure; Segis- 
mundo is overbearing and disrespectful, he delivers rude speeches, 
commits deeds of violence, and is promptly given a sleeping potion and 
hurried back to prison. Here he is told that he has been asleep, and 
that he has merely dreamed of the brilliant reception that was ac- 
corded him. As time goes on Segismundo gradually learns to control 
himself; he develops into a normal human being, succeeds with the 
help of supporters in defeating his father (to whom he submits in filial 
fashion), is proclaimed King, and rules with justice toward all his sub- 
jects. 

In Enciso’s drama Carlos is also represented as a wild and irresponsi- 
ble youth, who is finally kept a prisoner in his own room. Here he falls 
into a raging fever from which he is cured (according to the original 
version of the drama) by S. Diego of Alcala. 

Is it unlikely that Calderén had Enciso’s drama in mind? The shift- 
ing of the scene from Spain to Poland is comprehensible: the royal 
house of Spain could offer no objections to the treatment of the theme 
with foreign characters. We are to remember that Racine (no. 8) and 
Campistron (no. 13) also shifted the scene of action to foreign coun- 
tries. 


6. JUAN P£REZ DE MontatvAn, El Segundo Seneca de Espana. Ap- 
peared first in 1632. 

The title, as can readily be supposed, refers to King Philip II. The 

other principal characters of the play are Don Carlos, Don Juan of 

Austria, Alba, Lady Leonor (who is in love with Don Juan), Santoyo 

(an old counsellor to the King), Pompeyo (a sculptor), and a Cardinal. 


ine) Frederick W. C. Lieder 


The plot is simple. In the first act Philip, in consultation with 
Santoyo, disposes of a number of petitions. Among those that are not 
granted is one from the people of the Netherlands; Philip decides to 
crush the uprising by appointing Alba as the commander of the Spanish 
forces in Flanders. In this act the love affair between Don Juan and 
Leonor is touched upon briefly. 

In the second act we find Carlos attempting to kill Alba, whose ap- 
pointment as commander has aroused the Prince’s jealousy. Unsuc- 
cessful in his attempt, Carlos tries to enlist the support of his uncle, 
Don Juan. During the heated argument that ensues they draw near 
Leonor’s house, whereupon Carlos loudly announces that he wishes to 
call on her. Juan calls Leonor to the window, Carlos addresses her, she 
cuts off his entreaties by slamming the window in his face, and Juan 
escorts the raging and threatening Prince back to the palace. Here the 
King orders his son to bed. 

In the third act the King, who has had many reports of his son’s 
violent actions, summons Carlos, reprimands him, and threatens to 
deal harshly with him unless he reforms. Carlos starts to upbraid Alba, 
but before finishing his speech he falls into a violent fever. The King 
gives orders to have his son carried to his apartments. Later, the 
Prince requests, through Don Juan, to be allowed to go to Alcala for 
his health, and the King gives his permission. During this act Philip, 
at the request of the Cardinal, signs the marriage agreement with Anne 
of Austria, orders the sculptor Pompeyo to make bronze figures for the 
garden of San Lorenzo, journeys to Segovia to meet his fiancée, and 
arranges for his fourth marriage on the following day. 

Montalvan, friend and admirer of Lope de Vega, had a short life; he 
was born in Madrid in 1602, and died, while insane, in 1638. His El 
Segundo Seneca de Espana, like all his works, was extremely popular. 
Cabrera’s Historia (1619) and Enciso’s El Principe Don Carlos were un- 
doubtedly his chief sources. Like Enciso, and unlike most later drama- 
tists, he follows historical facts. The action takes place in 1570. Here 
we find a slight deviation from history, for Carlos plays a part in the 
drama, though the historical Carlos died in 1568. Nowhere in the 
drama do we find any hint that Philip robbed his son of a bride. As in 
Enciso’s work, Carlos is represented as a stubborn, overbearing, and 
intemperate youth. Montalvan’s delineation of the characters of 
Philip and Carlos is excellent. His play, however, is inferior to Enc- 
iso’s. 


The Don Carlos Theme VAT 


7. CESAR VICHARD Satnt-REaL, Dom Carlos, Nouvelle Historique, 
Amsterdam, 1672. 


St. Réal has been treated thoroughly in the thesis by Happel and in 
the monumental work by Dulong. Without question St. Réal’s story, 
a curious mixture of fact and fiction, had an influence such as few books 
have had. Three editions of the work appeared in Amsterdam in 1672 
and two in 1673, one in Paris in 1673, another in Lyon in 167s, followed 
by many more editions in the following two centuries. It was quickly 
translated into English and German. It was the main source for the 
important treatments of the theme by Otway, Campistron, Alfieri, and 
Schiller, who, in turn, influenced many other writers. 

Dulong (I, 118-129) mentions twenty sources that St. Réal might 
have utilized. He concludes with the significant statement (I, 128): 


Saint-Réal s’est dispensé de ce travail. Il cite confusément au bas des 
pages les historiens frangais et protestants, presque tous ardemment hostiles 
a Philippe II, et les historiens espagnols ou italiens qui lui sont favorables. 
I prend dans les textes, a droite et a gauche, non ce qui lui parait plus vrai, 
mais ce qui s’adapte le mieux a son plan, ce qui lui permettra le mieux de 
rehausser l’intérét de sa narration par des épisodes piquants. En un mot, il 
n’a cure de la vérité historique et ne cherche que l’intérét romanesque. Et 
la ot les données soi-disant historiques de ses auteurs lui paraissent laisser 
des lacunes, il y supplié de sa belle imagination. 


St. Réal was clever to see that the love of Carlos for his stepmother 
and former fiancée, whether historically true or not, would appeal to 
popular fancy, and this element in the story he emphasized again and 
again. He was not mistaken in his judgment. Most of the writers who 
later treated the theme accepted without hesitation St. Réal’s touching 
account of Carlos’s consuming passion for his father’s wife. 

We need not dwell on the familiar details of St. Réal’s tale — the 
marriage of Philip to Elizabeth, the meeting of the Prince with the 
Queen, the intrigues of Eboli and Don Juan, the suspicions of the King 
about the relations of his wife to his son, the plans of Carlos to go to 
Flanders, the discovery of the plot, the death of Carlos (who opens his 
veins in the bath) and of the Queen (who drinks poison). 

The influence of St. Réal not only on fiction but also on historical 
accounts is best summed up by a biographer of Don Carlos. W. Mau- 
renbrecher (Don Carlos [Berlin, 1869], p. 5) gives the following re- 
markable account: 


12 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


Ein franzdsischer Dichter, Saint-Réal, ist es, der dieser Aufgabe geniigte. 
Mit leichtem und pikantem Tone gab er das Bild, das als das iiberlieferte bis 
heute gilt. Saint-Réal ist es, der aus eigenen noch dazu falsch gedeuteten 
Angaben die riihrende Liebesgeschichte zusammengezimmert hat, die reich- 
liche Thriinen aus schénen Augen hervorzulocken pflegt. Der historische 
Roman, den er als Geschichte ausgab, fand den allgemeinsten Beifall; er 
ward iibersetzt und gelesen allenthalben in Europa. Und noch heute stésst 
der Forscher in spanischen Bibliotheken — ich rede aus eigener Erfahrung 
— bisweilen, und nicht gerade selten, auf sorgfaltig gehiitete Manuscripte, 
die als die eigentliche wahre Aufklarung iiber Don Carlos ihm geboten wer- 
den: mit Spannung schlagt er auf; er ist erstaunt, nichts als eine Copie oder 
eine Uebersetzung des schon oft gedruckten Romans von Saint-Réal zu lesen. 
So sehr ist man in Spanien noch von der untriiglichen Wahrheit jenes Trug- 
bildes iiberzeugt. 


8. JEAN Racine, Mithridate. Tragédie, Paris, 1673. 


In the Preface to his drama, Racine makes the following statement: 
“ Ainsi je ne pense pas qu’il soit besoin de citer ici mes auteurs. Car, 
excepté quelque événement que j’ai un peu rapproché par le droit que 
donne la poésie, tout le monde reconnaitra aisément que j’ai suivi 
Vhistoire avec beaucoup de fidélité.”’ 

The principal characters in the drama are: Mithridate, King of 
Pontus; Monime, affianced to Mithridate and already proclaimed 
Queen; Pharnace and Xipharés, his sons by different mothers; Arbate, 
a close friend of Mithridate and governor of one of the provinces; 
Phaedime, confidante of Monime; Arcas, a servant of Mithridate. 
Both Pharnace and Xipharés are in love with Monime, their father’s 
betrothed; Monime, however, confesses her affection only for 
Xipharés. In the wars between Pontus and Rome Xipharés loyally 
supports his father, while Pharnace is in secret alliance with the 
Romans. At the end of the play Mithridate, before dying, commends 
the faithful Monime to the care of Xipharés. 

Have we in Racine’s drama a parallel to the traditional story of Don 
Carlos, Philip, and Elizabeth of Valois? We might identify Philip with 
Mithridate, Monime with Elizabeth, Xipharés with Don Carlos, the 
war of Pontus against Rome with the war of Spain against the Nether- 
lands. Monime, to be sure, is affianced, not married, to Mithridate, 
but she has been proclaimed Queen; moreover, it was against the tra- 
ditions of the French stage in Racine’s day to represent on the stage 
the love affairs of married ladies. There are other points of similarity. 
Monime discourages Xipharés in his suit; Elizabeth deliberately avoids 


The Don Carlos Theme ig 


encouraging Carlos. Xipharés has a rival in his half brother Pharnace; 
Carlos (according to St. Réal) has a rival in Don Juan of Austria. 

St. Réal’s Dom Carlos, Nouvelle Historique first appeared in 1672; two 
other editions were published in 1672, and three in 1673, the year in 
which Racine’s Mithridate was brought out. St. Réal’s story became 
popular immediately. Whether Racine had an opportunity to see the 
printed text of St. Réal’s novelette before starting to write his Mzthri- 
date is difficult to determine, for Racine’s drama was published in 
January, 1673, and a notice of its coming production had appeared as 
early as August, 1672, in the Mercure galant (III, 270). Dulong (II, 
79), however, thinks that Racine might have become acquainted with 
St. Réal’s work even before it was published: 

Le garde des sceaux . . . n’en veut pas permettre l’impression, mais il s’est 
élevé a la cour une véritable cabale en faveur de ce livre. D’autre part, nous 
savons par un petit écrit contemporain que Saint-Réal avait donné lecture 
de son ouvrage avant de le publier, 4 divers amis. II n’est impossible que 
Racine, qui parait avoir connu personnellement Saint-Réal ait, avant le mois 
d’aotit 1672, assisté a l’une de ces lectures, entendu parler tout au moins d’un 
ouvrage qui fit quelque bruit avant méme d’étre imprimé, et qu’il ait été 
amené de cette maniére a concevoir une tragédie dont le sujet serait, comme 
dans la nouvelle de Saint-Réal, la rivalité amoureuse d’un pére et d’un fils. 


I regard as of extreme importance the statement from Racine’s 
Preface (quoted at the beginning of this item). Racine says that every- 
one will recognize that he has faithfully followed history. Did everyone 
know of the love affairs of Mithridates, of his sons, and of his fiancée? 
Or was Racine thinking of a more widely known story, namely that of 
Don Carlos? Did he, like Campistron (no. 13), transfer the scene and 
give other names to his characters in order not to offend the sensibil- 
ities of the Spanish Court? This hypothesis seems not improbable. 


9. DER UNGLUCKLICH-VERLIEBTE FUrst: oder Prinz Karls auss 
Spanien kuriz-gefasste Liebes-, Lebens-, und Todes-Geschichte. Aus 
dem Franzisischen verteutscht. Im Jahr MDCLXXIV [1674]. 

The Preface, ‘An den Teutsch-gesinneten Leser,” is signed “J. H. 

W. v. B.” The British Museum catalogue suggests that these are the 

initials of “‘ Johann Hermann Widerhold von Basel.” 

The author, whose source is undoubtedly St. Réal, states his am- 
bition in the Preface: 


Mein Abschen ist allein dieses, was andere vor mir gethan und noch thun, 
fortzusetzen, nemlich auss Liebe zu der Mutter-Sprach, das reine Teutsche 


14 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


unter den Annehmlichkeiten der Geschichte dem Leser so viel fiiglicher 
beyzubringen. 

This story — especially the opening paragraphs — may be regarded 
as a paraphrase rather than as a translation of St. Réal. Of interest is 
the fact that it appeared only two years after St. Réal’s story was first 
published. Apparently it is the first adaptation of St. Réal in a foreign 
language. 


10. THomas Otway, Don Carlos, Prince of Spain. A tragedy, London, 
1676. 

This play, appearing only four years after St. Réal’s novelette, is the 

first English treatment of the story as found in St. Réal; it must be 

looked upon as one of the most important treatments of the theme in 

any language. It is written in heroic couplets, but otherwise reminds 

one strongly of Shakspere, particularly of Othello. 

Otway’s treatment of the material of St. Réal is masterly. The play, 
moreover, was strikingly successful. The Biographica Dramatica gives 
interesting citations from a letter of Mr. Booth to Aaron Hill, from 
Dr. Johnson, and from Rochester’s Trial of the Poets for the Bays. It is 
of interest that Otway was one of the few dramatists (Campistron, 
nine years later, was another) who followed St. Réal closely in the 
matter of the catastrophe; Carlos opens his veins in the bath. The 
Queen dies of poison. The King suffers from remorse. 

Otway was only twenty-five when he wrote Don Carlos, the most 
remunerative of all his writings. Like Dryden and other English men 
of letters, he was influenced by the French classical drama. In Don 
Carlos, therefore, the so-called three unities seem to be consistently 
maintained. 

St. Réal’s story, ‘‘Englished by H. J.,” appeared in London in 1674 
— two years after the original French version was published. St. Réal 
visited London in 1676, the year in which Otway’s drama was pub- 
lished and first performed (at the Duke’s Theatre). 


11. G. Brp1oo, Karel, Erf-Prins van Spanje; Treurspel, 1670. 
The copy in the Barton—-Ticknor Collection of the Boston Public 
Library was published at Amsterdam in 1753. A Preface, ‘“Aan de 
Weleedel geboorene Juffer Mejuffer Aletta van Outshoorn,” is dated 
1679 and signed “‘G. Bidloo.” 

The play is divided into five acts, covers eighty-two pages (less 
than 2000 lines), and is written in rhymed Alexandrines. There are 


The Don Carlos Theme 15 


thirteen characters, the principal ones being Philip, Isabella of Valois, 
Karel, Rodrigo Gomez de Silva, and Anna de Mendoza Cerda. There 
is no character corresponding to Alba. 

The chief source seems to be St. Réal. A letter from the Queen re- 
ceived at Alcala by Carlos arouses the King’s suspicions. Carlos, under 
cross-examination by Philip’s guards, “heeft zyn ring in’t lief ge- 
zwolgen.” One of the guards, Fresnada, gives Carlos a sleeping potion; 
he knows it is poison, but drinks it and dies. 


12. DANIEL CASPER VON LOHENSTEIN. A so-called ‘‘Heldenbrief” on 
Philip and Eboli, Breslau, 1680. 
Lohenstein lived from 1635 to 1683. His Trauer- und Lustgedichte 
and his Sémtliche Gedichte both appeared in 1680. In a collection of 
Lohenstein’s poems entitled Blumen (Breslau, 1680), we find two 
“Heldenbriefe” (pp. 49-57) that deal with Philip and Eboli. The first, 
entitled “Konig Philip an die Fiirstin Eboly,” written in rhymed 
Alexandrines, contains one hundred lines; the eleventh line — ‘‘Sohn 
und Gemahl seh ich mit trocknen Augen sterben” — refers to the 
tragic fate of Carlos and Elizabeth. The second letter, “Eboly an 
Konig Philippen,” likewise contains one hundred rhymed Alexan- 
drines. Eboli is represented as having only one eye. This seems to be 
the only literary reference to this deformity, although at least one por- 
trait (attributed to Alonso Sanchez Coello and preserved in the collec- 
tion of the Duke of Pastrana) depicts Eboli with a pad over one eye. 
Max Freiherr von Waldberg (Die galante Lyrik, Strassburg, 1885) 
gives to Hoffmannswaldau the credit for having introduced into Ger- 
man literature the so-called ‘‘ Heldenbrief,’’ and states that Lohenstein 
and Mencke wrote ‘‘Heldenbriefe” on the Don Carlos theme. 


13. JEAN GALBERT DE CAMPISTRON, Andronic, Paris, 1685. 


Like Calderén and Racine (nos. 5 and 8), Campistron found it ad- 
visable to shift his scene from Spain (this time to Byzantium) and to 
give his characters non-Spanish names. The Emperor Collojean 
Paléologue corresponds to Philip II, his wife Iréne to Elizabeth of 
Valois, his son Andronic to Don Carlos, his ministers Léon and Mar- 
céne to Alba and Domingo, and Martian to Posa. The action hinges 
on Andronic’s love for his stepmother, Iréne, and on his desire to con- 
ciliate the Bulgars (who correspond to the Netherlanders in other 
plays). The prince is placed under arrest by his father and dies by 
opening his veins while in the bath. The Empress takes poison; the 
Emperor is overcome with grief. 


16 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


Campistron states in the Preface to Andronic (edition of 1715): 


Je concus la premiére idée de ce sujet sur une histoire moderne, écrite par 
M. l’abbé de Sainte-Réal, et qui a été, pendant plusieurs années, entre les 
mains de tout le monde; mais comme, par des raisons invincibles, je ne 
pouvois pas mettre sur la scene les personnages de M. de Sainte-Réal sous 
leurs véritables noms, je fus obligé de chercher ailleurs quelque €vénement 
qui ressemblat 4 celui qu’il avoit traité. Je trouvai heureusement ce qui je 
cherchois dans l’histoire de Constantinople. Les caractéres de Collojean, 
d’Andronic et d’Iréne sont les mémes que M. de Sainte-Réal a donnés 4 ceux 
dont il a parlé; et les faits des deux histoires sont conformes dans toutes leurs 
circonstances. . . . Le succés de cette tragédie fut aussi heureux a la cour et 
a la ville, qu’aucun qu’il y ait jamais eu; et il ne passa méme, pendant les 
premiéres représentations, des choses si avantageuses pour moi, qu’il ne me 
convient pas de les rapporter. . . . Ce succés a été si grand qu’il auroit pu me 
persuader que j’ai fait une piéce parfaite, si j’avois été plus vain que je ne 
suis; mais, bien loin de le penser, j’avoue de bonne foi qu’il y a plusieurs dé- 
fauts: ainsi j’attribue sa réussite autant a la beauté du sujet et 4 l’adresse des 
acteurs, qu’a mes vers et a mes pensées. Le sujet est le plus touchant et le 
plus singulier qui ait jamais été traité. 

Andronic is arranged in five acts, is written in rhymed Alexandrines, 
and is comparatively short (about 1700 lines). Although usually 
classed as one of the great dramatic treatments of the Carlos theme 
(the dramas by Enciso, Otway, Alfieri, and Schiller are the others), 
Campistron’s Andronic lacks force and conviction. As an example we 
might cite the final lines, spoken by the Emperor, after he learns that 
his son and his wife have died: 


Estoient-ils innocens ou coupables tous deux, 
Je ne scais: mais helas! que je suis malheureux! 


Speaking of the brevity of Andronic, Dulong (II, 93, 94) sums up 
the play thus: 


Mais on voit trop que cette brévité n’est que pauvreté et stérilité d’inspira- 
tion. Avec un admirable sujet, Campistron n’arrive vraiment ni 4 émouvoir 
ni a intéresser. On sent, en lisant son Andronic, combien les régles classiques, 
si elles sont un stimulant pour les écrivains de génie, se changent en pesantes 
entraves pour les médiocres. Campistron n’a su placer l’intérét de sa tragédie 
ni dans Vhistoire, ni dans la peinture de l’amour et de la jalousie. Il substitue 
a Vhistoire une politique conventionnelle et banale. Et l’amour, nous l’avons 
vu, n’occupe presque jamais qu’une place secondaire dans cette tragédie ot 
la passion devrait tout enflammer. 


14. JOHANN BURKHARD MENCKE (nom de plume, Philander van der 
Linde), ‘“Heldenbriefe” on Don Carlos and Elizabeth of Valois. 
Written 1692(?); published 1705(?). 


The Don Carlos Theme By 


Mencke’s dates were 1674 to 1732. Max Freiherr von Waldberg (Die 
galante Lyrik, p. 133), says: “‘Mencke behandelte auch den Stoff von 
Don Carlos und Isabella”; Koberstein (Geschichte der deutschen Na- 
tional Literatur, II [ed. Bartsch, Leipzig], 213, n. 15): “‘Friiher da 
er [Mencke] noch zu sehr in Hoffmannswaldau’s Gleichnisse verliebt 
war, hatte er sich diesen, namentlich in seinen meist im 18. Jahre ver- 
fertigten Heldenbriefen, die im ersten Theile [der Gedichte] stehen, zum 
Vorbild genommen.” 

The date of composition of the “ Heldenbriefe’’ — if Mencke wrote 
them in his eighteenth year — would be about 1692, and the publica- 
tion about 1705 (the first edition of the Galante Gedichte). 

The Columbia University Library has an edition of 1723. Under the 
title ‘“‘Heldenbriefe”’ we find the heading “‘Liebe zwischen Carl dem 
Infanten von Spanien und seiner Stieffmutter Isabella.” A prose In- 
troduction of one page tells of Carlos’s imprisonment and of the Queen’s 
death from poison. Mencke undoubtedly used St. Réal as his source. 
Then come two letters, the first “‘Isabella an ihren Stieff-Sohn Carl 
Infanten von Spanien” (72 lines), the second “‘Carl an seine Stieff- 
mutter Isabella Kénigin von Spanien” (68 lines). These ‘‘Helden- 
briefe’”’ are written in rhymed Alexandrines with the rhyme scheme 
ababcdcd, in which a and ¢ are feminine, 6 and d masculine. 


15. De HisToriscHE LEVENS- EN LIEFDENSVERHAAL VAN CAREL 
ERFPRINS VAN SPANJEN. War in met verwonderenswaardige 
zeldsaamheden het huwelyk met de Princesse van Vrankryk aange- 
toond werd; en hoe die Princesse door do dood van Carels Moeder, 
door zyn Vader pepretendeert en verkregen woerd. Alles met een korte 
beknopte Historische Verhaalinge door een voornaam Liefhebber der 
waare beschryvinge byeengebragt, Amsterdam, 1737. 

This work (it consists of 186 pages) follows closely the story of St. Réal, 

and is listed here mainly because the “ voornaam Liefhebber der waare 

beschryvinge”’ seems to be willing to have the public believe that the 
work is original with him. There is a copy of the work in the Staats- 
bibliothek in Munich. 


16. ANDRONICUS, TREURSPEL. Gefolgd naar het Fransche van den 
Herre de Campistron, Te Amsteldam, 1741. 

The ‘‘Toewying aan de Kundige Oefenaren der Nederduitsche Dicht- 

kunste”’ (a poem of 12 rhymed Alexandrines) is signed “F.v.Steenwyk, 

de Jonge.” 


18 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


The Dutch version, like its French model, is written in rhymed 
Alexandrines, and follows the original closely. The arrangement of 
scenes, however, differs. Campistron’s acts are divided as follows: 
Act I, eight scenes; II, eight scenes; III, ten scenes; IV, twelve scenes; 
V, thirteen scenes — total, fifty-one scenes. Steenwyk’s acts are di- 
vided: Act I, seven scenes; II, seven; III, nine; IV, nine; V, eight — 
total, forty scenes. 

Steenwyk’s arrangement is more logical than Campistron’s. For 
example, the Dutch version avoids scenes of only a few lines. One of 
Campistron’s scenes, in fact, has less than one full line. In Act I, 
Scene ii, Crispe appears, Léon exclaims ‘‘Que l’on nous laisse seuls,”’ 
Crispe leaves the stage, and a new scene begins. 

There is a copy of Steenwyk’s play in the British Museum. 


17. Marquis A. L. bE XimEeNnES, Dom Carlos. Tragédie en cing actes 
et en vers. Représentée pour la premiere fois sur le Thédtre de Lyon, 
le Mardi 5 Mai 1761. 


The copy in the British Museum does not give the name of the author 
or the date and place of publication. The catalogue, however, states 
that the work was published at La Haye in 1761. 

The scene is laid in Madrid in 1568. There are only nine characters 
— Philip, Elizabeth, Carlos, Alba, Egmont, the Duchess of Mont- 
morency, Elvira (a confidante of Eboli’s), the Marquis de Bergh (a 
friend of Carlos’s). The play is written in rhymed Alexandrines, and 
is comparatively short (about 1600 lines distributed over 80 pages). 
Thus it is less than one third as long as Schiller’s drama. 

An “Avertissement,” below the list of actors who took part in the 
first performance, reads: 


Tout le monde scait que Monsieur de Capistron [sic} traita le sujet de Dom 
Carlos sous le nom d’Andronic: mais on a cru que |’avanture tragique & 
vraie de cet infortuné Prince d’Espagne pourrait faire une impression neuve 
& distincte de tout ce qui s’appelle Art et Seduction de Roman. Du reste on a 
taché de s’éloigner également d’une imitation servile, & de l’affectation su- 
perbe d’éviter les ressemblances nécessaires dans le méme sujet. 


Ximenés’s play is a mediocre piece of work, and shows throughout 
that Campistron’s Andronic was the main source. Ximenés deserves 
credit for discarding the Byzantine names employed by Campistron. 
Ximenés’s play also ends differently from Campistron’s — Carlos 
throws himself upon his sword and dies. It is of interest that in three 


The Don Carlos Theme 19 


of the prose versions of Schiller’s drama (Ba, Bd, and Bs) Carlos 
stabs himself. 

Contemporary opinion of Ximenés’s play was distinctly unfavorable. 
Referring to one of the early performances, Rousseliére (p. 26) makes 
the statement: “Au nombre des spectateurs se trouvait fougueux cri- 
tique Diderot. Nous avons dans ses oeuvres l’analyse qu’il fit de la 
piéce: c’est un jugement d’une sévérité 4 faire frémir quiconque se méle 
de composer des vers.” 


18. JOHANN GOTTFRIED Dyk, Don Carlos und Elizabeth. Trauerspiel, 
1770. 

This play is based on Thomas Otway’s Don Carlos, Prince of Spain 
(London, 1676). (See Goedeke, Grundriss, V, 2, § 253, 11.) Unable 
to find the book in any of the libraries that I have consulted, I am in 
doubt whether to class it as a translation or an adaptation. The fact 
that Dyk uses a title different from Otway’s seems to indicate that his 
work is an adaptation. 


19. José DE CANizarES, El Principe Don Carlos. Written about 1720, 
published at Valencia in 1773. 


The volume in which the play appears (there is a copy in the British 
Museum) is entitled ‘Comedia Famosa. El Principe Don Carlos de 
Don Diego Ximenez de Enciso.”” At the end of the play appears the 
statement: ‘‘Con Licencia: En Valencia . .. Afio 1773.” 

For more than a century it was not known that this play was not en- 
tirely the work of Enciso. Rudolph Schwill (‘The Comedias of Diego 
Ximenez de Enciso,”’ Modern Language Notes, XVIII, 194-210) had 
pointed out that the final scenes of the later edition are different from 
those of the first. J. P. W. Crawford (“El Principe Don Carlos of 
Ximenez de Enciso,” Modern Language Notes, XXII [Dec. 1907], pp. 
238-241) showed that the version of 1773 is based on a manuscript by 
Cafiizares. It should be noted that Adolf Schaeffer (Geschichte des 
spanischen Nationaldramas, I (Leipzig, 1890], 400) anticipated the 
discovery made seventeen years later by Crawford. Speaking of the 
jater version, Schaeffer remarks ‘‘dass dagegen die Ueberarbeitung 
(wahrscheinlich von anderer Hand, vielleicht von Canizares) unsers 
Wissens nur in dem Einzeldruck Valencia 1773 vorkommt.”’ 

Schaeffer, in translating Enciso’s drama (no. 95), utilizes the tragic 
ending of the final scene in the version of Cafiizares. Carlos is not 


20 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


miraculously cured (as in Enciso’s version), but dies in a fever — the 
result of his own excesses. 


20. FRANCESCO BECATTINI, Don Carlos Principe di Spagna, 1774. 


The play, in a manuscript at the Biblioteca di Parma, has the date 
February 22, 1774. A short description may be found in Ezio Levi’s 
article ‘“‘Gli Antecedenti del Filippo dell’ Alfieri” (in the Rassegna 
Bibliographica della Letteratura Italiana, vol. XXI, no. 12, pp. 347- 
357): 

The day after the marriage of Philip to Isabella the King’s special 
agent, Duke Alba, arrests Carlos for plotting with Egmont to aid the 
rebels in Flanders. The King asks the advice of a grand council of 
nobles. Opinions are divided, but the final decision is in favor of the 
death sentence. 

Becattini’s drama is apparently the first Italian treatment of the 
Don Carlos theme; it was written nine years before the publication of 
Alfieri’s Filippo (no. 22) and ten years before the publication of Pepoli’s 
La Gelosia Snaturata (no. 24). It is doubtful whether Alfieri and 
Pepoli, when they wrote their plays, had any knowledge of the exist- 
ence of Becattini’s. 


21. PIERRE FRANCOIS ALEXANDRE LEFEVRE, Elizabeth de France. 
Accepted by the Comédie Francaise in 1781. 


Owing to the opposition of the Spanish ambassador, the play was not 

produced until two years later, when it was given the new title of Don 

Carlos. It will be discussed in more detail under no. 23. The fate of 

Elizabeth de France is humorously described in the following passages. 
Rousseliére says (p. 25): 


La permission d’exposer son ouvrage aux applaudissements ou aux sifflets 
du parterre vient d’étre refusée 4 M. Lefévre. .. . Son Elizabeth de France a 
été renvoyée par le censeur ordinaire au jugement de M. lieutenant de police, 
—par M. lieutenant de police a M. le garde des sceaux, — par M. le garde 
des sceaux 4 M. de Vergennes, ministre des affaires étrangéres — et par 
celui-ci 4 M. comte d’Aranda, ambassadeur d’Espagne. Le comte d’Aranda 


obtint que la piéce ne serait pas jouée publiquement. Elle ne le fut que sur 
un théatre particulier. 


Madame de Staél (De I’ Allemagne, Pt. 2, chap. 17) is also caustic in 
her remarks about the attitude of the Spanish ambassador: 


Plusieurs écrivains ont traité ce sujet en France; mais on n’a pu, dans 
ancien régime, le mettre sur le théAtre; on croyait que c’était manquer 


The Don Carlos Theme 21 


d’égards a l’Espagne que de réprésenter ce fait de son histoire. On deman- 
dait 4 M. d’Aranda, cet ambassadeur d’Espagne connu par tant de traits qui 
prouvent la force de son caractére et les bornes de son esprit, la permission 
de faire jouer une tragédie de Don Carlos, que l’auteur venait d’achever, et 
dont il espérait une grande gloire. “Que ne prend-il un autre sujet?” répondit 
M. d’Aranda. — Monsieur l’ambassadeur, lui disait-on, faites attention 
que la piéce est terminée, que |’auteur y a consacré trois ans de sa vie. —

ments dans l’histoire? Qu’il en choisisse un autre. — Jamais on ne put le 
faire sortir de cet ingénieux raisonnement, qu’appuyait une volonté forte. 


22. Vittorio ALFIERI, Filippo IT, Siena, 1783. 


Alfieri, well-known Italian writer of tragedies, lived from 1749 to 1803. 
As he himself states in his Vita, his Filippo IT (which was first written 
in French) is based on St. Réal’s story. 

The plot is singularly concise. There are only six principal char- 
acters — Philip, the Queen, Carlos, and three grandees (Gémez, Pérez, 
Leonardo). As in St. Réal’s account, Carlos is in love with his step- 
mother and former fiancée; also, he is in sympathy with the rebels of 
the Netherlands. Pérez, who corresponds in a way to Schiller’s Posa, is 
murdered by the King’s orders. In the last scene Philip decrees that 
Carlos shall die. He offers the Prince a choice between poison and 
dagger. Carlos chooses the latter, and stabs himself. Philip decides 
that his wife is to continue to live, but she tears the dagger from her 
husband’s hand and stabs herself. 

Alfieri’s drama is undoubtedly one of the most important treatments 
of the Carlos theme. Of grim interest is the bloodthirstiness that the 
author attributes to Philip. Carlyle states bluntly: ‘Alfieri’s Filippo 
is perhaps the most wicked man that human imagination has con- 
ceived.” Of interest, too, is Carlyle’s comparison of the methods of 
Schiller and of Alfieri in their treatments of the theme: “The mind of 
one is like the ocean, beautiful in its strength, smiling in the radiance 
of summer, and washing luxuriant and romantic shores; that of the 
other is like some black unfathomable lake placed far amid the melan- 
choly mountains; bleak, solitary, desolate; but girdled with grim sky- 
piercing cliffs, overshadowed with storms, and illuminated only by 
the red glare of the lightning. Schiller is magnificent in his expansion, 
Alfieri is overpowering in his condensed energy; the first inspires us 
with the greater admiration, the last with the greater awe.” (Life of 
Friedrich Schiller (London, 1825], p. 123). 


22 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


23. Don Cartos. Tragédie de M. Le Feévre, lecteur et secrétazre ordi- 
naire de S. A. E. Monseigneur le Duc d’Orléans, premier prince du 
sang, représentée en 1783 par les Acteurs de la Comédie Frangoise sur 
le théatre de Son Altesse Sérénissime, Bruxelles, 1784. 


In 1781 Lefévre’s Elizabeth de France was accepted by the French 
Academy, but was not produced, owing to the objections of the Spanish 
ambassador (no. 21). Lefévre made changes in the drama; under the 
new title, Don Carlos, it was played twice at the private theatre of the 
Duke of Orleans. In 1820, seven years after the death of Lefévre 
(1741-1813), it was produced publicly for the first time (at the Odéon). 
The play is included in M. Petitot’s Répertoire du Thédtre Frangotis 
(vol. VI, Paris, 1817) with an account of the author and a short criti- 
cism of the play. 

Lefévre’s Preface of about three pages, largely taken up with a dis- 
cussion of Campistron’s Andronic, begins with the statement: “Les 
particularités de la vie et de la mort de don Carlos sont entre les mains 
de tout le monde,” and ends with the following tribute to the appeal 
made by the Don Carlos theme: ‘‘ Andronic a eu quarante représen- 
tations de suite; et certainement le succés d’Andronic n’étoit que le 
succés du sujet: nul homme de lettres n’en doute aujourd’hui.”’ 

Lefévre’s drama contains about two thousand lines, is written in 
thymed Alexandrines, and is divided into five acts. The principal 
characters are Philip, Elizabeth, Carlos, Alba, Gémez, Lerma, Don 
Fernand (an emissary of the Flemish people and disguised as one of 
Philip’s guards), Eugenie (a French attendant of the Queen). Carlos 
and the Queen are suspected by the King, courtiers assure the King 
that his wife and his son are in love, the King learns that Carlos has 
sent a letter to the Queen, and decides that Carlos.must die. Finally, 
when the King actually reads the letter, he realizes that Carlos had 
planned to flee to the Netherlands in order to defend his father’s in- 
terests. The King exclaims: ‘‘Qu’ai-je lu? Chaque mot les absout et 
m/’accuse.”’ His repentance, however, has come too late. A curtain is 
drawn aside at the back of the stage, and Carlos, a dagger at his feet, 
appears bleeding and dying. He forgives his father, and exhorts Eliza- 
beth to live. As he dies, Elizabeth falls at his feet. She dies of despair. 
The play ends with the words of the King: 


O ma femme! o mon fils! innocentes victimes! 
Mais puisque c’en est fait et qu’il me faut régner, 
Cachons a l’univers que j’ai di pardonner. 


9 


The Don Carlos Theme Ze 


These lines, like the whole of the drama, show that Lefévre was able 
to improve upon the model that he seems ever to have had before him 
— Campistron’s Andronic. 


24. ALESSANDRO PEpojt, La Gelosia Snaturata o sia D. Carlo, Infante 
di Spagna; Tragedia. Con tre lettere sopra le quattro prime tragedie 
del Conte Alfieri, la prima della quali é la proposta del Conte Pepoli 
al Sig. Raineri de’ Calsabigi, la seconda é la riposta del suddetto, e 
Pultima la contra risposta del primo coll’ offerta della presente 
tragedia, Napoli, 1784. 


One of the severest critics of Alfieri’s works was Alessandro Pepoli of 
Bologna (1758-96). He was convinced that he could write a better 
drama than Alfieri’s Filippo (no. 22), and published his Lu Gelosia 
Snaturata a year after the appearance of Alfieri’s drama. Pepoli’s 
work, as was to be expected, called forth ridicule on the part of Alfieri. 

The play is comparatively short (74 pages), and has only eight char- 
acters — Filippo, Carlo, Elisabetta, Alva, Gémez, Montigni, Don 
Giovanni, and Eleonora. The first four acts take place in the royal 
palace at Madrid and the fifth act in prison. Curiously enough, the 
stage directions are given in footnotes and in an ‘‘Avvertimento agli 
attori” of six pages following the list of characters and preceding the 
text of the play. 

Pepoli’s Philip is far more humane than Alfieri’s; in fact, in Pepoli’s 
play the King hesitates to condemn Carlos and the Queen until the 
arch-conspirators, Alva and Don Giovanni, convince Philip that his 
throne is in danger. Alva reads to Carlos and Elisabetta the sentence 
that the King has pronounced; he hesitates to shed blood, but they 
must die nevertheless. Although the lines of the play are rather ob- 
scure, Pepoli’s directions are not: the prison is to be blown up, and the 
prisoners are to be buried under the ruins. In the ‘“Avvertimento” 
(pp. 113-114) Pepoli describes how the catastrophe is to be brought 
about. 

Lo scoppio non potra essere espresso che da tre o quattro carabine caricate 
a semplice polvere, e sparate al di sotto del palco. . . . In quanto poi alla 
rovina essa deve esseguirsi col precipizio della finte volte, e pareti anteriori, 
e formare un ammasso, che riempia il dinanzi della Scena, e acceni gia sepolti 
i due amanti, che si trovano dietro di questo ammasso naturalmente nas- 
costi. 


There is a copy of Pepoli’s play in the Harvard University Library.

25. Lours-SEBASTIEN MerctER, Portrait de Philippe II, Roi @ Es- 
pagne, Amsterdam, 1785. 

This drama, written in prose, tells the traditional story of Don Carlos 
in fifty-two loosely connected scenes (the play is not divided into acts). 
These scenes, however, can be divided into five unequal groups which 
deal respectively with (1) the love of Carlos for Elizabeth and the 
King’s concern about his wife and son; (2) the description of the auto- 
da-fé which the members of the Court attend; (3) the political plans 
and schemes of Philip II; (4) the arrest, condemnation, and death of 
Carlos (he is turned over to the Inquisition); and (5) the death of the 
Queen (she is poisoned by order of the King). 

The two chief sources followed by Mercier are St. Réal’s novelette 
and Robert Watson’s The History of the Reign of Philip the Second, King 
of Spain (London, 1777) (a French translation of which also appeared 
in 1777). Like some other dramatists (particularly Alfieri), Mercier 
depicts Philip in the darkest colors. The King is represented as a hypo- 
crite and cruel monster. Carlos, on the other hand, is imbued with all 
the idealistic aspirations of the latter part of the eighteenth century. 
Dulong (II, 122) remarks dryly: ‘‘D. Carlos, cela est clair, a lu avec 
profit Bayle, Voltaire et ’ Encyclopédie.” 

Mercier’s drama is of interest because Schiller translated the Intro- 
duction (Précis historique), and published it in his Thalia (Heft 2[1786], 
pp. 71-104) in connection with the earliest version of the first three 
acts of his own Don Carlos. It is doubtful whether Schiller owes much 
to Mercier; he had read St. Réal and Watson before he translated 
Mercier’s Introduction. 


26. DER UNGLUCKLICHE Don Cartos, EINE WAHRE GESCHICHTE ZUR 
WARNUNG FUR HOFLINGE. Nach dem franzisischen frei bearbeitet, 
Leipzig und Wien, 1786. Other editions, 1787, 1795. 

This is again a version of St. Réal’s story, and, as in so many other 

cases, the unnamed author is willing to let the public believe that his 

account is original with him. (See August Hettler, Schillers Dramen. 

Eine Bibliographie (Berlin, 1885]; also Goedeke, Grundriss, V, 2, 

253, I.) 

27. FRIEDRICH SCHILLER, Dom Karlos, Infant von Spanien. Ein 
dramatisches Gedicht, Leipzig, 1787. 


Other editions appeared in 1799, 1801, 1804, 1805. In the edition of 
1801 the form “Dom” was changed to “Don”; recent editions, par- 


The Don Carlos Theme 25 


ticularly the “Saikular Ausgabe,” employ the spelling “Carlos” in- 
stead of “Karlos.’”’ 

The earliest version of the first three acts (stopping at the end of 
Act ITI, Scene ix) appeared in the Thalia between 1785 and 1787, and 
contained four thousand one hundred and forty lines. The first com- 
plete version of 1787 had six thousand two hundred and eighty-two 
lines; the version of 1801 had five thousand four hundred and forty- 
eight lines; that of 1805, the basis of later reprints, had five thousand 
three hundred and seventy. 

Schiller’s most important source is undoubtedly St. Réal’s Dom 
Carlos, Nouvelle Historique; this is indicated by Schiller’s own state- 
ments in the “Vorrede in der Rheinischen Thalia” and in numerous 
references in his Briefe iiber Don Carlos. Other sources are Robert 
Watson’s History of the Reign of Philip II, Ferreras’s history of Spain, 
Gregorio Leti’s life of Philip. 

In a letter to Reinwald (April 14, 1783) Schiller states that his Don 
Carlos has its soul from Shakspere’s Hamlet, the blood and nerves from 
Leisewitz’s Julius von Tarent, and its pulse from Schiller himself. 
Other literary influences can be readily detected — other plays of 
Shakspere’s (especially Othello), Lessing’s Nathan der Weise and Emilia 
Galotti, some of the classical dramas by Corneille and Racine, Montes- 
quieu’s L’ Esprit des Lois. Some commentators are fairly convinced 
that Schiller was acquainted with Otway’s Don Carlos and with 
Campistron’s Andronic; Schiller, who never hesitated to acknowledge 
his indebtedness to others, mentions neither of these authors. 

“ Schiller’s drama is the best known, and likewise one of the most im- 

portant, treatments of the Don Carlos theme. What impresses one 
most is Schiller’s intuition that the popular ideas about King Philip 
must be revised — that Philip is a sincere, even though misguided, 
human being, and not a cruel monster.”Schiller’s Carlos, on the other 
hand, differs in every respect from the historical Carlos; Schiller fol- 
lows St. Réal in depicting the Prince in love with his former fiancée, 
now his father’s bride. This element in the story appealed strongly to 
almost all those that treated the theme. 

Aside from the various editions of Schiller’s play in blank verse, 
there are four prose versions — Br and Bs (the so-called Pérez versions, 
in which insults to Catholicism are avoided), and Ba and Bd (known 
as the Domingo versions). In the blank verse editions and in the prose 
version Br, Carlos is turned over to the Inquisition; in Ba, Bd, and Bs, 


26 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


Carlos stabs himself. All of Schiller’s versions have a catastrophe dif- 
ferent from that in St. Réal, where Carlos opens his veins in the bath. 


28. Dom Kartos, INFANT VON SPANIEN. Trauerspiel in 5 Aufsiigen, 
fiir die Biihne bearbeitet von B —1 und B —r, Augsburg, 1790. 


This play is contained in volume VI (Zweiter Jahrgang) of the Deutsche 
Schaubtihne. This collection of plays may be found in the British 
Museum. A set has been recently acquired by the Harvard University 
Library. 

For many reasons this version of the theme is one of the most inter- 
esting. It is the only one, for example, in which King Philip stabs his 
own son. 

The list of characters differs from Schiller’s in that the Countess 
Fuentes, the Grand Inquisitor, and the Prior of the Carthusian mon- 
astery are omitted. Act I has eight scenes instead of nine. Act II, 
which has twelve scenes instead of fifteen, substitutes for Schiller’s 
line (spoken by Domingo) “‘Trotz sei geboten allen Kénigen” a line 
spoken by Alba, ‘‘Trotz sei geboten dem mutigen Knaben und der 
schénen Franzésinn,’’ — thus omitting Scenes xiii, xiv, and xv of 
Schiller’s play. Act III has nine scenes instead of ten; it introduces, 
after the soliloquy of the King, a scene between Karlos and Posa, and 
it excludes Schiller’s ninth scene and the soliloquy of Posa. Act IV, 
with seventeen scenes instead of twenty-four, excludes Scenes i-vi, 
viii, xiv, and xxiv. Act V, with ten scenes instead of eleven, omits 
Scene vii, makes two scenes of Schiller’s Scene ix, and omits Scene x 
(the King’s interview with the Grand Inquisitor). 

At the end of the play the Queen sinks into Karlos’s arms. Then 
follows the tragic stage direction, ‘‘ Kénig zieht rasch sein Schwert, und 
ersticht seinen Sohn,” whereupon Karlos falls, then cries out ‘‘Gott! 
rettet die K6nigin! sie ist unschuldig!” and dies. The final stage direc- 
tions are: “Die Kénigin ohnmachtig — alle Granden in Bewegung — 
der Konig lasst das Schwert sinken — Der Vorhang fallt.” It is diffi- 
cult to imagine a more forceful and tragic ending. 


29. IsABELLA: Tragedia di Gaetano Fedele Polidori da Bientina, 
Londra, dai torchi di A. Grant, No. 91, Wardour Street, Soho, 
MDCCXC [1790]. 


Polidori was for a number of years secretary to Alfieri. In 1789 he 
became involved in revolutionary schemes in Paris, and received from 


The Don Carlos Theme yy | 


Alfieri a letter full of reproaches. Thereupon he went to London, where 
he lived until his death in 1835. 

In 1790 Polidori published his tragedy Isabella, a copy of which, 
along with a friendly letter, he sent to Alfieri. The latter never replied. 
The reason for Alfieri’s silence is not difficult to understand. Polidori 
had largely plagiarized Alfieri’s Filippo IT (no. 22). This is brought 
out clearly by Ezio Levi (Il Principe Don Carlos nella Leggenda e nella 
Poesia [Rome, 1924], pp. 347-349). 

The end of Polidori’s drama is tragic. Carlos, in prison, stabs him- 
self with a dagger which is offered to him by Isabella. Isabella there- 
upon drinks a vial of poison. 


30. ALESSANDRO PEPOLI, Carlo ed Isabella. Tragedia, Parma, 17092. 


This play is a revision of La Gelosia Snaturata (no. 24), the presen- 
tations of which, though praised in the main, had been criticized un- 
favorably on account of the “‘terribile scoppio della mina col precipizio 
del carcere.’”’ One of Pepoli’s friends, the Abbot Cesarotti, in a letter 
of September 15, 1791 (quoted by Pepoli in the Preface of Carlo ed 
Isabella) commented enthusiastically on the effectiveness of the final 
scenes in Pepoli’s earlier play. 

In Carlo ed Isabella Gémez is substituted for Alba in the final scene, 
the character of the King and the malicious motives of the conspirators 
are brought out more clearly, the music that closes the acts is revised, 
and the scenery rearranged. In deference to Cesarotti, however, the 
incomprehensible catastrophe — the blowing-up of the prison — is 
retained. 

Pepoli was not inclined to underestimate his own importance. In the 
Preface to his revised drama he puts the question of his future fame as 
follows: 

Altro a dir non mi resta, se non che il solo io non sono fra gli Italiani vi- 
venti che abbia trasportato al teatro questo argomento. 

Uno fra noi vi sara meglio riuscito. 

Qual egli sia, tocca a voi giudicare.

32. GESCHICHTE DES SPANISCHEN INFANTEN Don Car os, Hof, 1795. 


The unnamed author mentions in his Preface his sources — essen- 
tially the same that St. Réal cites in the footnotes of his story. The 
author admits his indebtedness to St. Réal: “Der Verfasser wahlte den 
Gang der Erzahlung den dieser [St. Réal] befolgt, weil er es war, bey 
dem er die Anzeige jener Quellen fand, und der auch selbst diese 
Geschichte sehr sch6n bearbeitete.” 

In the Preface the author mentions also a book that he had the good 
fortune of seeing in Vienna — found among the writings that the Em- 
peror Charles VI brought from Madrid and probably written by a so- 
called heretic who was burned at the stake. This book contains a severe 
arraignment of the Grand Inquisitor Espinosa. 

The book, while admittedly following St. Réal, is not a translation. 
Frequently the author paraphrases his source, and the material of the 
original has been expanded. A copy of the book (it contains 191 pages) 
may be found in the British Museum. 


33. ANONYMOUS ENGLISH VERSION, 1795. 


The Biographica Dramatica states that the first English translation 
of Schiller’s drama was published in 1795, but it does not give the 
name of the translator. A reviewer of an English translation of Fresco 
(Monthly Review, Il [1795], 311) seems to imply that the translation 
of Don Carlos is by the same person who translated Fresco; he goes on 
to say that Don Carlos is quite an epic poem in dialogue, and he hopes 
that other pieces of the Germans will be naturalized by the same hand. 

Since a copy of the 1795 version is not available, one has difficulty in 
deciding whether it is to be listed as a translation or an adaptation. 
The author of the 1822 version (no. 53) states in his Preface (p. x) that 
the subject of the tragedy has been often treated; then he mentions 
among others ‘‘an anonymous writer of 1795.”’ This seems to indicate 
that the 1795 version is an original treatment or an adaptation, prob- 
ably the latter. 


34. Witt1am Dunzap, Don Carlos. An adaptation of Schiller’s drama 
of the same name. Played at the Park Theatre, New York, 
May 6, 1799. 


Dunlap, who lived from 1766 to 1839, wrote and adapted for the stage 
at least sixty-six plays; of these, thirty are in print, thirty-six are not. 
Among the latter is Don Carlos. (See Oscar Wegelin, Early American 


The Don Carlos Theme 29 


Plays, 1714-1830 [New York, 1900]; Joseph N. Ireland, Records of the 
New York Stage from 1750-1860, 2 vols. [New York, 1866-67].) 

Dunlap’s Don Carlos is of greatest interest. It had only one pro- 
duction — the only time that Don Carlos was produced in English, 
either in England or in America, from the publication of Schiller’s play 
in 1787 until the performance of Mansfield’s version in 1905 (see no. 
104). 

Treland (I, 285) gives the following notice: 


May 6, [1799]. First time, Dunlap’s adaptation of Schiller’s Don Carlos: 


Philip IT Mr. Fennell 
Don Carlos Mr. Cooper 
Marquis Posa Mr. Martin 
Duke of Alva Mr. Hallam, Jr. 
Count Lerma Mr. Tyler 
Duke of Feria Mr. Miller 
Dominick Mr. Hogg 

Prior Mr. Perkins 
Princess of Eboli Miss E. Westray 
Duchess Miss Hogg 
Marchioness Miss White 
Elizabeth Miss Barrett 


The play was a failure and never repeated. 


A comparison of Dunlap’s list of characters with Schiller’s shows 
that Dunlap omitted the following: Farnese, Clara Eugenia, Fuentes, 
Medina Sidonia, Taxis, the Grand Inquisitor, and Mercado. Appar- 
ently the Inquisition plays little or no part in the drama. 

Dunlap’s adaptation must have been made hastily, for it was to be 
played as one of the benefit performances for the members of the com- 
pany at the Park Theatre in New York at the end of the season of 1799. 
In connection with the production of the play on May 6, Dunlap an- 
nounced: ‘‘Last night of performing for the benefit of the Lessee, the 
parties in whose name the bill of the night is made out, being hereafter 
alone responsible.” 

G. C. D. Odell (Annals of the New York Stage, Il [New York, 1927], 
55) has the following to say: 

Dunlap took a benefit on May 6th, providing a wretched performance of 
Don Carlos. . . . Dunlap says humorously that “this voluminous poem” the 
manager “‘curtailed, and it was more curtailed in the performance. The re- 


ceipts were 676 dollars. It was not repeated. It was unmercifully shorn of 
its beams.”’ With this performance Dunlap withdrew for the season.

36. Don Cartos, INFANT OF SPAIN. A Tragedy in five acts. Trans- 
lated from the German of Frederick Schiller. By Benjamin Thomp- 
son, Esq. London, 18or. 


I list this version (it is in prose) as an adaptation for two reasons: the 
renderings are free and rather tame, and the arrangement of the scenes 
differs from Schiller’s. Thompson numbers his acts, but not his scenes. 
Whereas Schiller has sixty-nine scenes in all, Thompson has only nine- 
teen (two in the first act, five in the second, three in the third, six in the 
fourth, and three in the fifth; see remarks on no. 82). 

Two examples will suffice to show Thompson’s colorless way of trans- 
lating. The last lines of the first act of Schiller’s drama (‘‘Arm in Arm 
mit dir, So fordr’ ich mein Jahrhundert in die Schranken’’) are trans- 
lated by Thompson: “‘Arm in arm with you, I bid defiance to tempta- 
tion and to danger.”’ The famous phrase in Act II, Scene ii (“‘ Dreiund- 
zwanzig Jahre, und nichts fiir die Unsterblichkeit getan!”’) becomes in 
Thompson ‘‘T am three and twenty years of age, and hitherto have 
been inactive.” 

There is a copy of this book in the British Museum. 


37- JOHANN WILHELM Rose, Carlos und Elizabeth. Trauerspiel in 
fiinf Aufziigen, Leipzig, 1802. 

The characters are Philip, Elizabeth, Carlos, Rui Gomez de Sylva, 
Don Juan of Austria, Spinola, Don Raymond, Pérez, Lerma, and an 
officer. It will be noted that several important characters of Schiller’s 
drama are omitted — Eboli, Posa, Alba, and Domingo. 

The play covers one hundred and fifty-four pages, is written in blank 
verse, and contains two thousand seven hundred and seventy lines 


The Don Carlos Theme eek 


(about half the length of Schiller’s play). Posa had been sacrificed be- 
fore the opening of the play. Carlos had been engaged to Elizabeth, 
but the King had married her instead. Carlos had been denied per- 
mission to go to Flanders except under Alba as commander. Various 
letters are found (as in Schiller), Carlos is suspected of disloyalty, is 
condemned to death, is allowed to select the method of dying, and 
chooses poison. Elizabeth comes to Carlos after he has taken the fatal 
draught, and he dies in her arms. 
There is a copy of the play in the Staatsbibliothek in Berlin.

39. MANUEL José QuintANA, El Panteén de Escorial. Dated April, 
1805. 

Quintana lived from 1772 to 1857. The above poem (it may be found 
on page 25 of the volume on Quintana in the Biblioteca de Autores 
Espanoles {Madrid, 1852]) contains about three hundred lines, and 
consists of a series of comments on past events by various historical 
characters — Don Carlos, Isabel de Valois, Philip II, Philip ITI, 
Philip IV, Charles II, and the Emperor Charles V. 


40. PHILIPS DE TWEEDE, KONING VAN SPANJE; TREURSPEL. Naar 
het Fransch Gefolgd. Door den Herr Mr. M. Straalman, Te Amstel- 
dam, MDCCCVI [1806]. 

A Preface (“Aan myne Landen Stadgenooten’’) states that inasmuch 

as the author’s first two plays (dealing with Greek subjects) had been 

received favorably, a third play — especially as it touches on the 
history of the Netherlands — might not be unwelcome. Since the 

Netherlands had suffered under Philip and had prospered under the 

House of Orange, the author presents this play in honor of the libera- 

tion of his country. 

Nowhere in the Preface is there any reference to the French source 
(the title page simply contains the phrase ‘‘naar het Fransch gefolgd”’). 
An interesting clue has made easy the solution of the riddle. After 
Carlos’s name in the list of characters there is an asterisk with a foot- 
note stating that “Karel was geboren uit het eerste huwelyk van 
Philips de tweede, zynde gehuwd geweest met Marie, dochter van Jan 


22 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


de Derde, koning van Portugal.’”’ In the drama on the Carlos theme 
by Ximenés (no. 17) is found a similar footnote: “Don Carlos était né 
du premier mariage de Philippe Second avec Marie fille de Jean III, 
Roi de Portugal.” 

Comparison of the French drama and the Dutch shows that Straal- 
man followed Ximenés fairly closely. Both dramas are written in 
rhymed Alexandrines. It should be remembered that the name of 
Ximenés did not appear on the title page of his drama, hence Straalman 
could hardly be blamed for simply using the phrase “‘naar het Fransch 
gefolgd.” 

There is a copy of Straalman’s play in the British Museum. 


41. Lupwic Treck, Der neue Don Carlos. Posse in dret Aktien, 1807 
or 1808. 


The manuscript of the play (it has never been published) is in the 
Staatsbibliothek of Berlin. It contains a parody of Schiller’s play and 
was written to be performed on the birthday of Tieck’s daughter Agnes. 

The plot is not deep. A certain official, Herr Einfalt, refuses to allow 
his daughter Réschen to marry a worthy fellow named Kaspar. A 
friend of the family persuades Einfalt to dig for treasure, and on this 
occasion causes the ghost of the deceased Frau Einfalt to appear. The 
ghost commands Einfalt to consent to the marriage. 

Meanwhile there appears in town a traveling company of actors, the 
director of which finally secures permission to perform the ‘‘moral- 
philosophical” play Don Carlos. In the fourth scene of the third act of 
Tieck’s play we find the beginning of the parody of Schiller’s. A few 
lines of this scene will suffice: 

Dominco: Die schénen Tage von Charlottenburg sind nun voriiber; und 
wir sind umsonst hier gewesen. 

Don Cartos: Was schleicht mir nur der Kerl so nach? Hialtst Maul, 
Monch! — 

Dominco: Mein Prinz, wenn meine Dienste — 

Don Cartos: Kurz und gut, ich will allein sein. — (Domingo ab.) O wie 
erschrecklich schrecklich schwermiitig bin ich! Ja, wohl ist solche 


Schwarmerei ohne Rat! Wen seh’ ich da? Wie, seh’ ich recht? Rodrigo 
kommt von seinen Reisen wieder? 


Tieck’s play ends happily for all concerned. Herr Einfalt marries 
one of the actresses. 


42. FRANZ GRILLPARZER, Blanka von Kastilien. Ein Trauerspiel in 
fiinf Akten. Written in 1808 and 1800. 


The Don Carlos Theme Be 


In 1806, when Grillparzer was fifteen years old, he became deeply 
interested in Schiller’s dramas. In his ‘‘Selbstbiographie” (Sauer’s 
edition of Grillparzer’s works, XIX, 37 ff.), Grillparzer indicates how 
greatly he was impressed by Schiller’s Don Carlos: 


Um diese Zeit waren mir auch die ersten Dramen Schillers in die Hinde 
gekommen, Die Rauber, Kabale und Liebe — Fiesko hatte ich auffiihren 
gesehen — und Don Karlos. Das letztere Stiick entziickte mich, und ich 
ging daran, auch ein Trauerspiel zu schreiben. Ich wahlte dazu aus der 
Geschichte Peters des Grausamen die Ermordung seiner Gattin, Blanka von 
Kastilien, und diese letztere gab den Titel her. Ich iibereilte mich nicht und 
schrieb ziemlich lange daran, wobei ich immer den Don Karlos im Auge hatte, 
mit dem es iibrigens auch zwei Fehler gemein hatte, dass ich namlich in der 
Mitte des Stiickes am Plan fnderte und es so ungeheuer lang geriet, dass 
man gut zwei volle Abende daran zu spielen gehabt hatte. 


Blanka von Kastilien is written mainly in iambic pentameter, and is 
almost as long as Schiller’s Don Carlos. (Blanka, in its final draft, has 
5125 lines; Don Carlos has 5370.) The three principal characters of the 
play are Don Pedro, King of Castile, Blanka of Bourbon, his wife, and 
Fedriko de Sussmann, a natural brother of the King’s. Blanka and 
Fedriko were in love before the King’s marriage to Blanka; Blanka, a 
victim of the schemes of diplomacy, had been married against her 
wishes. The King distrusts them both and finally has them murdered. 

How greatly Grillparzer was influenced by Schiller’s dramas, espe- 
cially by Don Carlos, is shown conclusively by O. E. Lessing (Schillers 
Einfluss auf Grillparzer [Madison, Wisconsin, 1902], pp. 103-162). 


43. Histor1A DEL PrincipE Don Carios Hijo PRIMOGENITO DEL 
Rey DE EspaNA PHELIPE II y DE DoNA Maria DE PorTUGAL. 
Nueva editién, Leipsich, 1810. 

There is no indication as to what editions preceded this one. The 

story contains the familiar motives found in St. Réal, who was un- 

doubtedly the chief source. The story proper (it contains 135 small 
pages) is followed by thirty pages of ‘‘Adiciones de un Autor Anony- 
mo a la Historia Antecedente.”’ Apparently the author of this work, 
like so many others, was unwilling to admit that he followed St. Réal. 

The only copy of the work that I have been able to find is in the 
British Museum. 


44. Francois IstporE Liquet, Phillippe II, tragédie en cing actes, en
