34 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


Liquet was director of the public library of Rouen and president of 
the Royal Academy of that city. His play was produced on February 1, 
1813, at the Théatre des Arts. According to Dulong (II, 139) it is an 
imitation of Alfieri’s drama. 

Rousseliére (p. 26 of his Introduction) says:,“‘M. Liquet n’eut pas, 
semble-t-il, beaucoup 4 se réjouir devoir sa piéce jouée au théatre de 
Rouen (1813).” 

Dulong gives the author’s name as “Frangois-Isidore Liquet”’; 
Rousseliére gives it ‘‘Th. Liquet.” 


45. Lorp Byron, Parisina. Dedication dated January 22, 1816. 


In editions of Byron’s collected works the poem is usually printed 
under the heading “‘Tales”’; it has five hundred and eighty-six lines, 
divided into twenty irregular sections and written mainly in rhymed 
iambic tetrameter. In the advertisement Byron says: 


The following poem is grounded on a circumstance mentioned in Gibbon’s 
“Antiquities of the House of Brunswick.” I am aware, that in modern times, 
the delicacy or fastidiousness of the reader may deem such subjects unfit for 
the purposes of poetry. The Greek dramatists, and some of the best of our 
English writers, were of a different opinion: as Alfieri and Schiller have also 
been, more recently on the Continent. The following extract will explain the 
facts on which the story is founded. The name Azo is substituted for Nicho- 
las as more metrical. (B.) 

“Under the reign of Nicholas III (A.D. 1425) Ferrara was polluted with a 
domestic tragedy. By the testimony of a maid, and his own observation, 
the Marquis of Este discovered the incestuous loves of his wife Parisina, and 
Hugo his bastard son, a beautiful and valiant youth. They were beheaded in 
the castle by the sentence of the father and husband, who published his 
shame, and survived their execution. He was unfortunate, if they were 
guilty; if they were innocent, he was still more unfortunate; nor is there any 
possible situation, in which I can sincerely approve the last act of the justice 
of a parent.” — Gibbon’s Miscellaneous Works, III, 470. (Ed. 1837, p. 830.) 


Three reasons may be adduced to show that Byron had Schiller’s 
Don Carlos in mind: in the first place, he mentions Schiller in his In- 
troduction ; secondly, in Byron’s poem (as in Schiller’s drama) only the 
son is punished; thirdly, in Byron’s poem (as in Schiller) the son main- 
tains that the stepmother was originally intended for him. This is 
brought out in lines 252-256 (in which the son, just before his execu- 
tion, addresses the father): 


The Don Carlos Theme 35 


Tis true that I have done thee wrong — 
But wrong for wrong — this, — deemed thy bride 
The other victim of thy pride, — 

Thou know’st for me was destined long; 

Thou saw’st and coveted’st her charms. 


46. Marie josepH CHENIER, Philippe II. Tragédie en cing actes, 
Paris, 1818. 

The editor of Chénier’s Oeuvres, in which the play was first published, 

remarks that it had been offered to the ThéAtre Francais at least fifteen 

years before, but had never been performed. Chénier was apparently 

not in good favor during the Napoleonic régime. 

The drama is skillfully put together. It opens with a scene in which 
Alba, who has just returned from the Netherlands to deliver a report, 
intimates that the Queen and Carlos are in love, and that Carlos is in 
sympathy with the revolt in Flanders. The King at first has faith in 
Carlos’s loyalty, although he admits that his son and his wife love one 
another. As the play develops, however, the King is gradually con- 
vinced by courtiers and by the Inquisitor Spinola that Carlos is guilty 
of treason. It is finally decided that Carlos is to die of poison. As he 
drinks the fatal goblet, the Queen appears, and reproaches Carlos for 
not letting her drink part of the poison. The King enters just in time 
to hear Carlos’s dying words: 


Pardonnons ...O mon pére. . . au nom de mes malheurs, 
Rendez la reine . . . heureuse, et vos sujets . . . je meurs. 


Although Chénier follows the tradition, to which St. Réal gave the 
impetus, that the Queen and Carlos are in love, he seems to lean on 
later treatments of the theme, rather than on St. Réal. Frequently his 
lines remind one of Schiller and of Mercier, and the final words of par- 
don are not unlike Otway and Lefévre. 


47. PuiLrppe II. Tragédie en cing actes et en vers. Par J.-B. Daumier 
(de Marseille), Paris, 1819. 

In the Preface Daumier states that his source was St. Réal. When he 
undertook the task of writing the drama, he knew so little about dra- 
matic literature that he was unacquainted even with the names of 
Schiller and Alfieri; the first treatment of the theme to come to his at- 
tention was the one by Campistron; but by that time he was writing 
the third act of his own drama. He expresses great admiration for 
Racine’s style. 


36 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


The characters are Philip, Elizabeth, Gémez, Spinosa, Palmése (a 
friend of Carlos’s who is the counterpart of Posa), Elvire (confidante 
of the Queen), Alvarés (captain of the guards). 

The play is written in rhymed Alexandrines, extends over sixty-eight 
pages, contains about fifteen hundred lines, and is divided into five 
acts. Carlos stabs himself; the Queen takes poison. 

Referring to Daumier’s play, Rousseliére (page 26 of his Introduc- 
tion) makes the interesting statement: ‘“‘M. Daumier, en 1819, ne put 
faire representer la sienne.” (See remarks under no. 76.) 

There is a copy of Daumier’s drama in the British Museum. 


48. Dre Doop vAN KAREL, KRONPRINS VAN SPANJE. Treurspel, in 
vif Bedrijven, Amsterdam, 1820. 


The anonymous author states in the ‘‘Voorberigt” (dated Decem- 
ber 3, 1819) that the story of Don Carlos deserves a dramatic treat- 
ment in Dutch, and that in the preceding year he had begun the task 
of writing a drama. Meanwhile he had got hold of Chénier’s drama on 
the same subject (it was published in 1818), had realized its excellent 
features, followed it in many respects, and adopted (though not liter- 
ally) the fifth act. The author makes clear, however, that his work is 
not a translation or adaptation of Chénier’s. The treatment of char- 
acters, particularly of the Cardinal Spinola, is different. 

The characters are Philip, Elizabeth, Carlos, Spinola, Gomes de Silva 
(Prince of Eboli), Joan van Glimes (Margrave of Bergen), Floris van 
Montmorencij (Lord of Montignij). The play consists of eighty pages 
containing about eighteen hundred rhymed Alexandrines. Carlos com- 
mits suicide by taking poison. The last lines of the play are spoken by 
Elizabeth; she vows that she will follow Carlos into death and be 
united to him. 

On pages 47 and 48 of the drama the author mentions among Flemish 
citizens the name of one of his own friends — Hendrik van Stralen. 

There is a copy of the play in the British Museum.

(1820).”

to obscurity, summarizes the plot briefly (II, 139).

de D. Carlos, seule partie de la piéce oti se monstrent encore quelques sou-

Elizabeth est morte depuis quelques années et que le roi lui-méme est re-

51. Dow Cartos, PRINCE Royal or Spain. A tragedy, translated and 
altered from the German, and adapted for the English stage by Simon 
Sabba, Paris, 1821. 


Sabba’s drama is an adaptation of Schiller’s, for it is cited by August 
Hettler (Schillers Dramen. Eine Bibliographie (Berlin, 1885], p. 19). 
Hettler does not state where he found a copy of Sabba’s work, and I 
have not been able to locate it in any library that I have consulted. 
Hettler states, however, that Sabba’s book was printed by Rosa in 
Paris in octavo form, and that it has four pages of introduction and 
one hundred and forty-eight pages of text. 


52. Lorp Joun Russet, Don Carlos or Persecution. A Tragedy in 
Five Acts, London, 1822. 


Russell begins his Preface with the remarkable statement: “I must 
confess that the two main props upon which the following attempt is 
built, have no solid foundation in history.”’ (He refers to the passion 
of the Prince for the Queen and to the part played by the Inquisition 
in bringing about the downfall of Don Carlos.) With reference to the 
Inquisition, Russell continues: ‘‘ That tribunal, however, has so many 
sins upon its head, that I can scarcely do it any injury by adding an 
imaginary one to the catalogue.” 

The play is written in blank verse, covers one hundred and sixteen 
pages (about 2300 lines), and has as principal characters Philip, Don 
Carlos, the Grand Inquisitor and his assistants, Osario (a follower of 
Don Carlos), the Queen, Leonora, and Luis de Cérdoba. 


38 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


Carlos, who is in love with the Queen, is accused of heresy and dis- 
loyalty by the agents of the Inquisition. He refuses steadily to betray 
his friends. Upon his escape from prison, he meets and kills Cordoba, 
but is himself seriously wounded. The King appears, Carlos asks for 
a drink, the King orders that this be given him. ‘The Grand Inquisitor 
puts poison in the drink, and Carlos swallows it. The King questions 
Carlos closely about the charges that have been made against him. 
Carlos denies his guilt so fervently that the King begins to weaken. 
At this point a messenger arrives from Rome with a letter which dis- 
closes that the Grand Inquisitor had deliberately plotted to arouse the 
King’s jealousy. Carlos dies, the King sentences the Grand Inquisitor 
to life imprisonment, the latter denounces the King. The play ends 
rather weakly with Philip’s words: 

May this sad story rest for ever secret! 


Vain hope! in one short day I have destroyed 
My peace of conscience and my hope of fame! 


John Russell, first earl of Russell, on two occasions Prime Minister, 
lived from 1792 to 1878. His Don Carlos, written at the age of thirty, 
had four editions in 1822, but was unsuccessful on the stage; it was an 
interesting venture in the field of literature by a man whose future 
fame was earned by his achievements in the field of politics. 


53. Don Cartos, A TRAGEDY. Translated and rendered into verse from 
the German of Schiller and adapted for the English stage, London, 
1822. 

This version, a copy of which may be found in the British Museum, 

has about three thousand lines — a little more than half the length of 

Schiller’s drama. The following characters of Schiller’s play are 

omitted — Farnese, Clara Eugenia, Fuentes, Feria, Medina Sidonia, 

the Prior, the Page, Mercado. One character not in Schiller’s list — 

Count Cordova — is included in this English version. 

The work is dedicated to Prince Augustus of Prussia (Versailles, 
October 16, 1820), because “‘you first taught me to admire the beauties 
of ‘Don Carlos,’ and to comprehend and render them faithfully.” 

The Preface of nineteen pages includes statements that are of ex- 
treme interest. Commenting on the fact that the French and English 
have condescended to translate and adapt a production of Schiller’s to 
their own stage, the author adds: “I cannot imagine how any man 
perusing the works of Schiller should have arrested his attention on 


The Don Carlos Theme 39 


‘Mary Stuart,’ when so admirable a production as ‘Don Carlos’ lay 
before him.” Then, a little later: “Schiller’s and Shakespear’s were the 
most gigantic of all mortal intellects in the art which they professed; 
the sight of a pigmy cannot reach the sublimity of their genius.” And 
again: ‘Shakespear never produced, on the whole, scenes so admirable 
as some to be found in the original of ‘Don Carlos.’” 

The author is strangely inconsistent in his method. At one point he 
says: “The difficulty of doing justice to ‘Don Carlos’ in translation 
will naturally occur to my readers.’ A little later he makes the re- 
markable statement: “I have found it necessary to retrench one half, 
consequently to omit many beauties, to leave out the under plot en- 
tirely, to make many changes, some additions, and to compose an en- 
tirely new catastrophe.” 

In spite of this drastic procedure, the author is bold enough to ex- 
press the following good opinion of his work: “In short, I avow, in my 
humble judgment, that I consider the ‘Don Carlos’ I now offer to the 
public (setting aside my own part, which is the versification), as the 
most perfect theatrical piece, in its construction, I am acquainted 
with.” 

The scenes between Carlos and Eboli (what the author probably 
means by the under plot) are omitted, also the scenes in which the 
destruction of the Armada is reported. Posa is not assassinated, but 
stabs himself. Carlos is turned over to the Inquisition. 


54. Don CARrtos, PRINCE D’ESPAGNE. Tragédie en cing actes, d’Otway, 
Paris, 1822. 

The Preface (“Notice sur Don Carlos’’) is signed “Jules Saladin.” 
His drama is in prose, whereas Otway’s is in heroic couplets. Saladin 
also breaks up the five acts into many scenes (Act I, nine scenes; 
Act II, eight; Act III, thirteen; Act IV, eighteen; Act V, eight — fifty- 
six scenes in all). In Otway’s drama, on the other hand, each act con- 
stitutes one scene. 

Saladin translates idiomatically and fairly closely. One statement 
in his Preface, however, deserves attention: “Il y a bien dans la tragé- 
die de Don Carlos quelques images vives, et des expressions peu ména- 
gées, que j’ai cherché 4 adoucir sans manquer 4 la litteralité dont je me 
suis fait une loi.” 

Because of this policy of Saladin’s and because of the arrangement 
of scenes, I list the work as an adaptation of Otway’s drama. There is 
a copy of the play in the British Museum. 


40 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


55. FRIEDRICH BARON DE LA Motte Fovugus, Don Carlos, Infant von 
Spanien. Ein Trauerspiel. Mit einer Zueignung an Friedrich von 
Schiller, Danzig (1823). 

A copy of this rare drama has been recently Wea by the Harvard 

University Library. Of special interest are penciled marginal notes in 

two different hands, one of which was written by a contemporary (on 

the last page appears the date 4/5, 1825). 

Fouqué’s drama, like so many of his productions, is a “tour de 
force.” Written mainly in blank verse, it employs elaborate rhymes 
again and again. It is inordinately long (six acts, 287 pages, more than 
5700 lines); in fact, it has about four hundred more lines than Schiller’s 
Don Carlos, which, if we except the trilogy of Wallenstein, is the longest 
of Schiller’s plays. 

The ‘“‘Zueignung”’ to Schiller consists of six stanzas in ottava rima, 
and invokes the favor of the master who had been dead eighteen years. 
In the last octave, Fouqué attempts to draw a comparison between his 
production and Schiller’s — a comparison which strikes one as amus- 
ing after one has read Fouqué’s play. Fouqué closes with the lines: 


Doch auch mein Carlos ist kein Ungeheuer, 
Und ist von deinem Carlos nicht so weit, 

Als es im Spiegel blitzt der Welterscheinung; — 
Und All’n uns lacht einst seelige Vereinung! 


After the phrase ‘‘nicht so weit” the commentator has written ‘‘als 
Tag von Nacht.” With this curt comment every reader of the play 
will agree. 

Of the many characters introduced in the play, the principal ones are 
Philip, Carlos, Elizabeth, Don Juan, Alba, Ruy Gémez (Carlos’s tutor), 
and two Flemish emissaries (Berg and Montigny). The scenes take 
place at Madrid, at Alcala, at Aranjuez, and in the monastery at 
Retamar. The time of action is not indicated; twice, however, Philip’s 
age is given as forty-four, which would fix the year as 1571 — three 
years after the death of Don Carlos! Fouqué evidently intended to 
have the action take place in the year of Carlos’s death, namely 1568. 

The plot is rambling. A number of references to Carlos’s cruelties 
and queer actions in his youth (at one point Carlos himself exclaims 
“ich bin zuweilen wunderlich’’), his readiness to duel with Fadrique, 
with Alba, even with his own father, his outbursts against many who 
come in contact with him — all these seem to lay the basis for the 
catastrophe that overtakes him. Curiously enough, Carlos exults 


The Don Carlos Theme 4I 


when he is told that Philip has married Elizabeth of Valois (to whom 
Carlos was supposed to be engaged). When Carlos, at the King’s re- 
quest, goes to Alcala to greet Elizabeth upon her arrival in Spain, he 
is overcome by her beauty and charm. 

The two envoys from Flanders, Berg and Montigny, after their re- 
quest that the Inquisition be forbidden in the Netherlands is bluntly 
refused by Philip, succeed in giving to Carlos a letter in which Egmont 
pleads for justice to his people. The King, who was on the point of 
yielding to Elizabeth’s plea that the army under Alba should not be 
sent to Flanders, changes his mind as soon as he learns that Carlos is 
involved in the plan. Alba is ordered to depart; Carlos exclaims “‘ Um- 
fasst mich! Flandern! Dein bin ich und Egmont’s!”’ 

Carlos is about to flee to Flanders, when he hears that the Queen is 
ill; he proceeds to Aranjuez, meets the Queen, and then rides wildly to 
Madrid. A message from the Queen begs him to do nothing rash. 
Meanwhile, the King follows Carlos to Madrid, enters his room while 
Carlos is asleep, gives orders that the Prince’s weapons be taken away 
and that the windows be walled up, and assigns to Gémez the responsi- 
bility of guarding Carlos. 

The sixth and last act isa strange mixture of incidents that border on 
the ludicrous. Philip calls a council to decide on Carlos’s fate. The 
Prince is charged with rebellion and with designs on the King’s life. 
After each member of the council gives his opinion — some advise life 
imprisonment, others that Carlos serve in a military campaign against 
enemies of the state — Philip dictates to his secretary, signs the docu- 
ment, and passes it for inspection to his councillors. The King has de- 
cided that Carlos must die; in doing so, Philip makes the remarkable 
statement (p. 256): 

Eins kommt auf des Infanten Hoheit selbsten an, — 
Das Eine, — welche Todesgattung er gebeut. 


Im Tod’ auch hiit’ er darin noch sein K6nigsrecht, 
Und wahle christlich, koniglich und ritterhaft. 


While the King is alone, the Queen (who is gravely ill and is supposed 
to be dying) enters the room, receives permission to visit Carlos, faints, 
seems to be dead, again opens her eyes, gets up, indulges in a speech, 
and walks out. Meanwhile, Carlos has decided that he will die by 
having his veins opened; a masked surgeon suggests that this be done 
in a bath, and Carlos accepts the suggestion. After Carlos has been led 
to the bath, the King decides that he must see his son once more — 


42 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


“Ich wollt’ ihm ja nur meinen Segen bringen!’’ Carlos, although 
bleeding to death, props himself up, and takes his departure from the 
world — after the King has extended his hand in blessing! Toward the 
end of the play announcements are made — that Egmont, Berg, and 
Montigny have been beheaded, that the Netherlanders and the Moors 
are in revolt, and that Carlos and the Queen have died. 

Fouqué, in spite of the “Zueignung,’’ has taken practically nothing 
from Schiller. From St. Réal he borrows the thread of the story — 
Carlos’s relations to Elizabeth, his sympathy with the revolt in Flan- 
ders, his condemnation and death by orders of his father. Apparently 
Fouqué drew upon his imagination to make his play different from 
every other treatment of the theme. He succeeded. If he had not 
labeled his work “ein Trauerspiel,’’ one might easily suppose that he 
had undertaken to write a burlesque. 


56. Dorcny pu Ponceau, Elizabeth de France, Tragédie. First pub- 
lished in Oeuvres Completes, Paris, 1826. 


This drama was written some years before it was published — prob- 
ably in or before 1820. Dulong (II, 140) says: “Il y en eut un autre 
[Don Carlos] d’un certain Doigni du Ponceau.” A footnote to this 
sentence refers to the ‘“Feuilleton dramatique de la Quotidienne, du 
22 décembre 1820.” 

Doigny du Ponceau was born in 1750 and died in 1830. In 1776 he 
was crowned by the Academy, but he was never elected to member- 
ship. In 1795 he helped to establish the Quotidienne, and thereafter 
he identified himself with the anti-republican movement. He wrote 
poems, epistles in verse, and tragedies — none of which were above 
the level of mediocrity. The fact that Dulong refers to him as “un 
certain Doigni du Ponceau”’ seems to indicate that he did not play 
an important part in the literary history of France. 

I have been unable to find a copy of his play. It is not listed in the 
catalogue of the Bibliothéque Nationale. 


57- ALEXANDRE SoumEt, Elizabeth de France. Tragédie en cing actes 


et en vers, représentée pour la premiere fois sur le thédtre francais, le 
2 mat 1828, Paris, 1828. 


As in Chénier’s drama (no. 46), Egmont, not Posa, is the friend who 
brings to Carlos the appeal of the Netherlanders. An interesting char- 
acter in Soumet’s drama is the hermit Alvarés, to whom the Queen 


The Don Carlos Theme 43 


confesses her love for Carlos. The details of the plot follow Schiller — 
the scenes describing ‘the conversation between Carlos and the Queen, 
between the King and Carlos, between Alba and the King, the episode 
about the Queen’s medallion, the announcement about the loss of the 
Grand Armada. In order to make the Queen admit her love for the 
Prince, the King informs her that Carlos is dead. The Queen is over- 
come; in a delirium she ardently confesses that she loves the Prince and 
hates her husband. Carlos and Elizabeth are given over to the Grand 
Inquisitor. The play closes with the words of the King: “‘Mon devoir 
est rempli; vieillard, faites le votre.” 

Soumet’s main sources are the plays by Schiller and by Chénier. If 
he knew St. Réal, his play gives no indication of such knowledge. Nor 
was an acquaintanceship with St. Réal’s story necessary; Soumet found 
in Schiller all the important facts that were utilized in his Elizabeth de 
France. 

One extremely interesting fact connected with the performance of 
Soumet’s play deserves mention. Rousseliére (p. 26) records the fol- 
lowing incident: 

L’ Ehizabeth de France de M. Soumet (1828) ne se soutint que pendant un 
petit nombre de soirées; cependant elle marque, dit-on, dans les souvenirs 
du Théatre-Francais par une innovation qui date de la. Précédemment une 
héroine, et méme un héros, ne devaient expirer que sur un fauteuil ou sur un 
canapé. L’actrice chargée du réle d’Elizabeth, Mlle Duchesnois, fit un coup 
de téte: elle osa, au grand scandale des vieux admirateurs de la tradition, se 
laisser tomber sur le parquet pour mourir. M. Soumet n’en fut pas plus 
applaudi. 


58. ANONYMOUS FRENCH VERSION, Paris, 1830. 

This version, with the most ridiculous ending imaginable, was per- 
formed in Paris in 1830, but was never published. The only reference 
to it that I have been able to find is in the Introduction to Rousseliére’s 
treatment of the theme. (See no. 76.) 

Rousseliére’s description (p. 24) follows: 

Je n’ai emprunté . . . pour finir, 4 la piéce qu’un ingénieux anonyme fit 
jouer (l’heureux mortel!) par les comédiens du Théatre-Frangais de Paris en 
1830. Cependant je note qu’il y a dans cette tragédie une émouvante in- 
vention pour précipiter la catastrophe du héros. On y voit au Ve acte Don 
Carlos prisonnier; un traitre lui persuade de fuir, en se coulant, a l’aide d’une 
corde, du haut du donjon royal ot le malheurex prince est renfermé. Carlos, 
émerveillé, se met naivement en chemin pour gagner le sol; mais 4 peine a-t-il 
depassé les premiers noeuds, que son atroce conseiller s’arme d’un coutelas 
et scie la corde vers le haut: bien entendu, l’enfant tombe dans les fossés et se 
brise!

60. ISABEL DE LA Paz. Drama en cinco actos y en verso de D. José 
Lorenzo Figueréa, Sevilla, 1839. _ 

The play is comparatively short — only ninety-six small pages. The 
principal characters are: Felipe II, Rey de Espafia; Isabel de Valois, 6 
de la Paz, su esposa; Amelia, confidente de Isabel; Carlos, Principe, 
hijo de Felipe II; Ruy-Gémez de Silva, Principe de Eboli, Ministro; 
Eduardo Gonzélez, Oficial de Alabarderos; four deputies from Flan- 
ders, and a number of minor characters. 

The scene is in Madrid, the time 1568. The first three acts are played 
in a room of the palace, the fourth in the house of Eduardo Gonzalez, 
the fifth in the room of the Inquisitor. Carlos is in love with the Queen. 
His friend Gonzalez (who plays a part much like that of Marquis Posa 
in Schiller’s drama) is murdered in Carlos’s presence by the King’s 
hirelings. As Gonzalez dies, Carlos cries ‘‘Muerto!” The play ends 
with the lines spoken by Montigni, one of the Flemish deputies: 

Dios no quiere venganzas en la tierra. 
A los tiranos da castiga el Cielo. 

Figuerda, like Schiller, is fair in his attitude toward King Philip. Of 
extreme importance, therefore, are the author’s remarks in his Preface 
(‘‘ Advertencia”’): 

He pintado el caracter de Felipe II como creo que era realmente. Astuto, 
sagaz, engafiador, ambicioso, adicto al absolutismo que crefa necessario para 
destruir los restos del sistema feudal, y para contenir la Heregia que en 
aquella época iba siempre unida 4 la rebelién, y 4 las espantosas guerras 
civiles que asolaron la Europa. Los Historiadores protestantes le han atri- 
buido la muerte de su hijo; pero eran enemigos jurados de este Monarca, y 
el odio les presté los pinceles con que apasionadamente hicieron su retrato. 


Sobre este punto no quiero insistir, porque es un error de la Historia ya 
desvanecido por Historiadores y criticos de alta nombradfa. 


A copy of Figuerdéa’s play may be found in the New York Public 
Library. 
61. FRANZ GRILLPARZER, Esther (1840?). 


The well-known Biblical story impressed Grillparzer with its dramatic 
possibilities as early as 1830. About 1840 he again took an interest in 


The Don Carlos Theme 45 


the subject, and began to work on a drama. It remained, however, 
a fragment. Only the first two acts and about fifty lines of a third act 
have come down to us; in all there are less than a thousand lines. 

Grillparzer was deeply interested in the Spanish drama, especially in 
the works of Lope de Vega. It is but natural, therefore, that commen- 
tators have seen in Lope’s La hermosa Ester a model for Grillparzer’s 
work. One scholar, W. Duschinsky of Vienna, argues ingeniously that 
Grillparzer, in planning his Esther, was again under the spell of Schiller’s 
Don Carlos. 

Duschinsky presents his case in an article Ueber die Quellen und die 
Zeit der Abfassung von Grillparzers Esther (Zeitschrift fiir die osterreich- 
ischen Gymnasien [November, 1899], pp. 961-973). He cites interesting 
parallel passages to show that there was much in common between 
Philip and Ahasver, Posa and Esther, Alba and Haman. 

Duschinsky’s theory seems plausible, particularly in view of the fact 
that in Blanka von Kastilien (see no. 42) Grillparzer consciously and 
admittedly used Schiller’s Don Carlos as a model.

65. ALEXANDRE SoumET, Le Secret de la Confession. (Théatre, édition 
définitive, pp. 29-66.) Paris, 1845. 
In an “avertissement’’? Soumet explains the purpose of his play. 
Le Secret de la Confession (titre défendu par la censure en 1827) est un 
de ces sujets-types, qui émanent de la civilisation moderne; il manquait a 
notre théAtre. On se souvient que Saint Jean Népomucene aima mieux 
mourir que de révéler les aveux dont une reine l’avait fait dépositaire: j’avais 
d’abord songé a4 le prendre pour le héros chrétien de ma tragédie; des con- 
sidérations artistiques m’ont engagé a le remplacer par le personnage d’Al- 
varés et 4 me servir de la fable de Don Carlos pour développer avec plus 
d’avantage mon idée premiére. Deux ou trois scénes de cet ouvrage sont 
imitées du Don Carlos de Schiller. Le réle d’Elizabeth de France avait été 
composé pour Mlle Duchesnois. Ce fut la derniére création de cette admirable 
actrice, méconnue quelques instants pour n’avoir pas voulu faire descendre 
son talent des hautes régions de l’art. 


Soumet’s Le Secret de la Confession is a smoother and more forceful 
version of the Don Carlos story than his earlier play, Elizabeth de 
France (no. 57). Some of the scenes are rearranged, and the fifth act 
is thoroughly revised. The character of Alvarés is brought out more 
clearly; whereas in the earlier version he succumbs to torture and re- 
veals the secret that had been confided in him, in the later version he 
continues to the very end to maintain that the Queen is innocent. In 
the later version, moreover, the Grand Inquisitor plays the dominating 
role that he plays in the final scene of Schiller’s drama. 


66. ALEXANDER Dumas, Le Page du Duc de Savoie, Paris, 1846. 


The scene of this novel is laid in the Netherlands in the time of the 
Emperor Charles V and in France in the time of King Henry II. The 
principal character is Emmanuel Philibert, Duke of Savoy, but there 
are interesting sidelights on the career of Don Carlos. It is evident 
from the following three passages that Dumas drew his material from 
St. Réal or from accounts that go back directly or indirectly to St. 
Réal. 

In Part 1, chapter 15 (“‘Aprés l’Abdication”’) occurs a conversation 
between Charles V and his grandson Don Carlos, as a result of which 
Charles advises his sister, Queen Eleonore to watch the boy carefully 


9 


The Don Carlos Theme 47 


and to report her deductions. She reports unfavorably. Then follows 
a significant paragraph by Dumas: “Ce petit bonhomme, qui n’eut pas 
fui 4 Inspruck, était ce méme don Carlos que son pére Philippe II fit 
tuer, douze ans plus tard, sous prétexte qu’il conspirait avec les revoltés 
des Pays-Bas.”’ 

In Part 3, chapter 2 (““L’Envoyé de Leurs Majestés les Rois de 
France et d’Espagne’’) there occurs, among the conditions of the treaty 
of peace, the following: “Que le roi Philippe épousera la princesse 
Elizabeth de France, qu’il avait d’abord demandée pour son fils don 
Carlos, et qu’avec cette princesse, il lui sera donné une dot de quatre 
cent mille écus d’or.”’ 

In Part 3, chapter 6 (“Le Colporteur’’) is found the significant 
passage: 

Hélas! il n’en était pas de méme de la pauvre Elizabeth! Fiancée d’abord 
au jeune prince don Carlos, qui lui avait envoyé son portrait, et qui avait 
recu le sien, elle avait vu la mort inattendue de Marie Tudor ruiner tout a 
coup l’échafaudage de son bonheur, qu’elle croyait hors de toute atteinte. 
Veuf de Marie, repoussé par Elizabeth d’Angleterre, Philippe II s’était re- 
battu sur Elizabeth de France, et, dans les conditions du traité de paix, on 
n’avait eu a changer que deux mots, qui devaient faire la malheur de deux 
personnes, et méme de trois. . . . Au lieu donc de ces deux mots: “‘ Le prince 
Carlos épousera la princesse Elizabeth de France,” on avait mis ces deux 
mots: ‘‘ Le roi Philippe épousera la princesse Elizabeth de France.”

68. E. M. Corman, Philippe II, Roi d Espagne. Drame en cing actes 
imité de Schiller et précédé de L’Etudiant d’ Alcalé, Paris, 1848. 
The play was first produced at the Théatre de la Gaité in Paris on 
May 14, 1846. Although Corman follows the main outlines of Schil- 
ler’s drama, he differs sufficiently to warrant calling his play a treat- 
ment of the theme rather than an adaptation of Schiller. The Pro- 
logue, for example, in which Carlos, disguised as a student of the Uni- 
versity of Alcal4, declares his love for the Princess Elizabeth in the 
royal park at St. Germain, has no counterpart in Schiller. The part 
played by Posa is taken by Egmont in Corman’s drama; Eboli becomes 
Ines, Domingo appears as Gaetano. As in so many treatments of the 


48 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


theme, the final scenes take place in prison. Carlos is shot (in Schiller’s 
play Posa undergoes this fate), and as he dies there enter his cell the 
King, the Queen, Alba, and numerous other courtiers. The Queen is in 
despair. The King bemoans the fact that he is alone on the most im- 
portant throne of the world. 

A Portuguese version (no. 70) of Corman’s treatment appeared in 
1851. The New York Public Library catalogue of this version states 
that the name Corman is a pseudonym for Pierre Etienne Piestre 
(1811-1903).

71. Don CARLOS, DER INFANTERIST VON SPANIEN, oder das kommt 
davon, wenn man seine Schwiegermutter liebt. Spanische Lokal posse 
mit starkem Berliner Beigeschmack und sehr vielen couplets, in drei 
lustigen Akten, frei nach Schiller, aber bedeutend verbessert. Musik 
von Gluck, Heyd’n, Lanner, Strauss, Meyerbeer, Gung’l und Mehr- 
eren. Auf dem Puppentheater der humoristischen Weihnachts- 
Ausstellung zu Berlin 104 Mal aufgefiihrt. Berlin, 1852. Verlag 
von Silvius Landsberger, Klosterstr. 41. Als Manuscript gedruckt. 


This interesting little farce (it covers only forty pages) has been re- 
printed by Gotthilf Weisstein in the “Berliner Curiosa”’ (no. 2). Ina 
“Nachwort”’ of twenty-two pages Weisstein gives an account of the 
author, who was no other than the publisher, Silvius Landsberger. 

The place of action is described thus: “‘ Der erste Akt spielt in Aran- 
juez, und nachdem die ‘schénen Tage’ daselbst ‘zu Ende sind,’ in 
Madrid.” The time is given as “zwischen 1651 und 1852.”’ The prin- 
cipal characters are: 


Philipp der Ixte, Kénig von Spanien. Etwas jahzornig und immer in Geld- 
verlegenheit. 

Hulda, seine linker Hand angetraute Gattin. Friither Putzmacherin in Paris 
und sehr geliebt von: 

Don Carlos, Kronprinz und Infanterist. Leidet sehr an Weltschmerz, und 
liebt, soviel der alte Philipp weiss, seine Stiefmutter hoffnungslos. 

Prinzessin von Eboli. Sehr heissbliitig und reizbar, sonst aber dusserst 
nachsichtig mit den Schwachen des mannlichen Geschlechts. 

Kasper, Friiher Aufwiegler in Berlin, jetzt Leibkoch bei der Prinzessin Eboli. 

Markis Posa von Castilien. (Angenommener Name.) Ist der Sohn eines 
jiidischen Irrenarztes aus Posemuckel. — Zweideutiger Character. 


Other characters are the Infanta Carlina, Alba, Domingo (who is de- 
scribed as the ‘“‘Prasident des Autodafeterklubs in Madrid’”’), and an 
Officer of the Noble Guard (who is referred to as “das rothe Ge- 
spenst”’). 

The plot moves quickly. Carlos is in love with the Queen, Eboli with 
Carlos. Kasper seems to be the general manager. The King gives 
orders that Carlos be hanged. Meanwhile he hears that the Queen has 


50 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


drowned herself. He asks Kasper to suggest an easy method of dying, 
and Kasper promptly says: ‘denn miissen Sie vor Langeweile ster- 
ben.” The King is interested in the suggestion, and Kasper begins to 
read from a newspaper various articles — ‘‘ Weihnachtswanderung von 
Ludwig Rellstab,” ““Verhandlungen aus der ersten Kammer,” “Ver- 
handlungen aus die zweete Kammer, Wahlangelegenheiten,” and then 
finally ‘““Wie man Hauser baut’’— whereupon the King expires. 
Carlos and Hulda, reports of whose deaths had been invented, fly into 
one another’s arms, Carlos is proclaimed King, he dismisses all the 
ministers except Posa, and announces “Ich werde mein Volk gliicklich 
machen.” 

The farce introduces many quotations from Schiller’s drama. It has 
also many clever references to political events of the times. It is 
written in prose interspersed with a dozen humorous poems which 
are sung to popular melodies. There is a copy of Weisstein’s reprint 
in the Harvard University Library.

73. PHILipp UND PEREZ. Historische Tragédie in fiinf Aufztigen von 
Karl Guizkow. (Als Manuscript nach den Dresdener Vorstellungen 
gedruckt), Dresden, 1853. 


The play, which is eighty-seven pages long, gives a list of more than 
thirty named characters in addition to grandees, courtiers, councillors, 
pages, monks, sharpshooters, and so on. The names of the actors and 
actresses of the Dresden performance are also given. The first act takes 
place at the Escurial, the other acts in Madrid. The time of action is 
1578. Gutzkow mentions two books that he considers of importance — 
L. Ranke, Fiirsten und Volker von Stideuropa, and A. Miguet, Antonio 
Perez et Philippe II, Paris, 1845. 

Although Don Carlos has been dead ten years before the action of 
this play begins, nevertheless his fate is referred to several times. (The 
passages in question will be quoted from a later and expanded version 
of the play; see no. 79.) 

Gutzkow gives his views about some of the important characters, 
especially of Alba and of Eboli. His account of the latter bears repe- 


The Don Carlos Theme 51 


tition: ‘‘Prinzessin Eboli ist in diesem Drama zehn Jahre alter als in 
Don Carlos. Sie muss liebenswiirdig und interessant, im Gespriich mit 
Philipp Akt I und Il.auch kokett sein, sonst aber durchweg den Ton 
der Grafin Orsina anschlagen.”’ 

There is a copy of the play in the New York Public Library. 


74. Don CARLOS AUF DER SCHMIERE. Date uncertain (1853?). 


In discussing the comic versions of the Don Carlos theme, Albert 
Ludwig (Schiller und die Nachwelt|Berlin, 1909], p. 343) mentions this 
play by an anonymous author. Since Ludwig cites Don Carlos auf der 
Schmiere just after he has called attention to Silvius Landsberger’s 
Don Carlos der Infanterist von Spanien (see no. 71), and since Lands- 
berger’s farce was first performed in the season of 1851-52, I place the 
date of the former at 1853 or thereabouts. 


75. Epuarp DEvrRieNnT. An acting version of Schiller’s Don Carlos. 
Karlsruhe, 1853 and 1867. 


A short account of this adaptation is given by Eugen Kilian in the 
Introduction of his own stage adaptation of Schiller’s drama (see 
no. 102). Kilian says (p. 5): 

Weit férderlicher waren die Versuche einzelner Biihnen, in der Kiirzung 
des Stiickes — abweichend von der Schablone — neue und selbststiandige 
Wege einzuschlagen. So brachte Eduard Devrient das Werk schon 1853 zu 
Beginn seiner Karlsruher Directionsfiihrung in einer Fassung auf die Biihne, 
die sich durch Herstellung der Verschwoérung zwischen Alba, Domingo und 
der Prinzessin, der Auftritte im Kartauserkloster und der einleitenden Szene 
des vierten Aktes vorteilhaft unterschied von der gangbaren Einrichtung der 
Tragédie. Einer Neueinstudierung im Jahre 1867 legte derselbe Biihnenleiter 
eine neue Bearbeitung zugrunde, die unter Beibehaltung der genannten 
Szenen erstmals den gliicklichen Versuch unternahm, in einer Reihe von 
Einzelheiten auf die Fassung der ersten Buchausgabe des Don Carlos von 
1787 zuriickzugreifen. 


76. AMEDEE DE LA ROUSSELIERE, Don Carlos. Tragédie en cing actes 
et en vers imitée de Schiller, Liege, 1855. 

There is a copy of this play, inscribed with the author’s name on the 

title page, in the private library of Alexander Liebisch of Leipzig. 

In an Introduction of thirty-three pages, Rousseliére gives his the- 
ories about adapting another man’s work. He follows Schiller’s plot 
in the main, but he expresses the greatest admiration for Otway and 
Alfieri. He states definitely that he owes nothing to Campistron, 


DUQUESNE UNIVERSITY LIBRARY, 07 


52 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


Ximenés, Lefévre, Chénier, Liquet, Daumier, Malinas, Soumet, Coo- 
mans, Lambinon, and to a certain anonymous author whose treatment 
of the theme was produced in Paris in 1830. 

This list is of extreme importance, for some of the authors are so 
obscure that it is difficult to find any other reference to them. In some 
cases the dramas were produced, but never published. 

Rousseliére’s work, in rhymed Alexandrines, is about two thirds as 
long as Schiller’s. As an example of his deviations from Schiller’s play, 
we may quote the closing lines. The Queen says to Carlos: “Non, 
espérez encore.” The Grand Inquisitor, in the last line of the drama, 
replies: “‘Son éspoir n’est qu’en Dieu!”

82. Don Cartos, INFANT OF SPAIN. Translated from the German of 

Schiller by Thomas Selby Egan. London, 1867. 
The translation is in blank verse, and is fairly smooth and accurate. 
I list this version as an adaptation of Schiller’s drama, because of 
Egan’s drastic method of reducing the: number of scenes. In Schiller 
the acts are divided into scenes as follows: Act I, nine scenes; II, fifteen 
scenes; III, ten scenes; IV, twenty-four scenes; V, eleven scenes — 
total, sixty-nine. Egan arranges his scenes as follows: Act I, three 
scenes; II, five scenes; III, three scenes; IV, nine scenes; V, three 
scenes — total, twenty-three. 

It is of interest to note that Benjamin Thompson, when translating 
Schiller’s drama into English (see remarks under no. 36), employed 
only nineteen scenes. 

Copies of Egan’s and Thompson’s renditions are in the British 
Museum. 


83. Don Cartos. OPERA EN Cinq Actes. Paroles de Méry et Ca- 
mille du Locle. Musique deG. Verdi. Représenté pour la premiére 
fois, a Paris, sur le thédtre Impérial de Opéra le 21 Mars 1867, 
Paris (1867). 

The characters are Philip, Carlos, Posa, a Prior, the Grand Inquisi- 

tor, Elizabeth, Eboli, Thibault (a page of Eboli’s), the Countess of 

Aremberg, Lerma, a Herald. 

The first act, curiously enough, takes place at Fontainebleau, where, 
in the forest, Carlos and Elizabeth meet. The second act is at St. Just, 
the third at Valladolid (the second tableau of this act is a “‘ Ballet de la 
Reine”), the fourth at Valladolid, the fifth at St. Just. Thus no part 
of the opera takes place in Schiller’s settings — Aranjuez and Madrid. 


The Don Carlos Theme as 


The story, however, is like Schiller’s. Carlos loves the Queen, she urges 
him to help the Flemings, the King interrupts their final interview and 
turns Carlos over to the Inquisition. 

_Verdi revised the opera from time to time. The most important 
change was the reduction from five acts to four. I have been unable to 
find any French copies of the four-act version. See the remarks under 
the next three items. 


84. Don Carto. OPERA IN Cinque Atti. Parole di Méry e Camillo 
du Locle. Musica di G. Verdi. Traduzione italiana di Achille de 
Lauziéres, Parigi (1867). 

Lauziéres follows the French version, which was published in the same 

year, but he does not translate closely. (See remarks under nos. 83 

and 85.) The chances are that the four-act Italian version was de- 

termined upon soon after the first production of the opera in French. 
There is a copy of this libretto in the British Museum. 


85. Don Cartos; AN OPERA IN Four Acts. The music by Verdi. 
The English Version by Thomas J. Williams. As represented at 
the Royal Italian Opera, Covent Garden (London, 1867). 


The Italian and English texts are printed side by side. Williams fol- 
lows an Italian text (of four acts) which differs from the Lauziéres 
version of 1867 (which has five acts) and from the Lauziéres and 
Zanardini version of 1884. Apparently, in the very year that the 
opera was first performed, there appeared a French version of five 
acts (Méry and du Loele), an Italian version of five acts (Lauziéres), 
an Italian version of four acts (which was probably the predecessor of 
the Lauziéres and Zanardini version of 1884), and Williams’s adapta- 
tion of the latter. 

The first act of Williams’s version and of his Italian model takes 
place at St. Just, the second at Madrid, the third at Madrid, and the 
fourth at St. Just. 

The stamp of the British Museum states that the book was received 
on July 12, 1867. The question then arises, Did Lauziéres or someone 
else make a four-act Italian version in 1867?

(See The Stage Cyclopaedia. A bibliography of plays. Compiled by

87. Gaspar NuNez pe Arce, El Haz de Lena. Drama en cinco actos 
y en verso, Madrid, 1872. 


In this drama, which has been called the most dignified Spanish play 
of the whole nineteenth century, the element that became so popular 
with most writers through St. Réal’s story — the love of Carlos for his 
stepmother and former fiancée, Elizabeth of Valois — is not to be 
found. The plot, in fact, shows little similarity with that of any other 
drama on the theme. 

At an auto-da-fé at Valladolid in 1559 Don Carlos de Sesa, a Cas- 
tilian nobleman who has been condemned to death, utters words of 
protest as he passes King Philip. The King, without losing his com- 
posure, replies: ‘‘If my son acted as you have done, I should not hesi- 
tate to carry to the pyre my bundle of wood in order to burn him.” 
These words are overheard by de Sesa’s son, who immediately plans 
to take revenge. With his sister Catalina he goes to Madrid, takes 
quarters in a modest little house, assumes the name Cisneros, becomes 
an actor, and plays the réle of buffoon. The eccentric Prince, Don 
Carlos, becomes interested in Cisneros. When envoys from Flanders 
come to Madrid in order to enlist Carlos for their cause, Cisneros plays 
the part of the intermediary, and induces Carlos to meet the envoys 
at the little house. While the negotiations are in progress, Cisneros 
decides that the moment for revenge has come; he hastens to Philip, 
reveals the plot, and guides the King and his courtiers to the scene of 
the conspiracy. Carlos is put under arrest. Meanwhile Catalina, who 
has fallen in love with Carlos, intercedes for him, and succeeds in per- 
suading the King to see his son again. Carlos, however, worn out by 
his excesses, has grown steadily weaker; the King forgives him, and 
Carlos dies repentant. The play ends with the cry of Cisneros: ‘Soy 
luterano!”’ 


88. ALFRED TENNYSON, Queen Mary, A drama, Boston, 1875. 


My copy of the book, published in Boston by James R. Osgood and 
Company, has the statement on the title page “‘ Author’s edition, from 
advance sheets.”’ The English edition appeared in the same year. 


The Don Carlos Theme 57 


The scenes in the play take place after the marriage of Philip of Spain 
to Mary Tudor, Queen of England (1554), and before the death of 
Mary and the proclamation of her half sister Elizabeth as Queen 
(1558). 

Tennyson indicates that Philip despairs of having an heir by Mary, 
that he realizes that Mary is in precarious health, and that he secretly 
hopes to marry Elizabeth Tudor after Mary’s death. To this end, he 
persuades Mary to declare Elizabeth her heir to the throne; he argues 
that if Elizabeth is not selected, Mary Stuart, Queen of Scots and wife 
of the dauphin of France, might succeed to the English throne. 

Philip also suggests Prince Philibert of Savoy as a husband for Eliza- 
beth — knowing, in advance, that Elizabeth will refuse to accede to 
this plan. Toward the end of Act V, Scene iii, in the course of a con- 
versation between Elizabeth and Feria, agent of Philip, there occur the 
lines: 

Elizabeth 
Perhaps; but we have seamen. Count de Feria, 
I take it that the King has spoken to you; 
But is Don Carlos such a goodly match? 
Feria 
Don Carlos, Madam, is but twelve years old. 
Elizabeth 


Ay, tell the King that I will muse upon it; 

He is my good friend, and I would keep him so; 
But — he would have me Catholic of Rome, 
And that I scarce can be; and, sir, till now 

My sister’s marriage, and my father’s marriages, 
Make me full fain to live and die a maid. 

But I am much beholden to your King. 

Have you aught else to tell me? 


These lines imply that Philip had instructed Feria to suggest Don 
Carlos as a possible match — again knowing that Elizabeth would not 
agree. Tennyson, however, has motivated the proposal poorly; no 
previous mention of Don Carlos by the King or by anyone else is re- 
corded in the play. Nevertheless, it is of interest that Tennyson, like 
so many others who touched upon the theme, believed Philip capable 
of using his son as a pawn to further the monarch’s selfish ends.

Das vorliegende Stiickchen ist — héherer Wahnsinn, aber — eine humo-

The play covers fifty-eight pages and is written mainly in rhymed

Toward the end of the play all the grandees kill themselves. Carlos is

Wir freuen uns zusamm’n 
Mit iibergrosser Miithigkeit,

The Don Carlos Theme 59 


go. BAYARD Taytor. An adaptation of Schiller’s Don Carlos. The 
manuscript is dated New York, January 14, 1878. 


In Mrs. Bayard Taylor’s book (On Two Continents; Memories of Half 
a Century, by Marie Hansen Taylor, with the codperation of Lilian 
Bayard Taylor Kiliani [New York, r90s5], pp. 271, 272) mention is 
made of a translation and adaptation of Schiller’s play. Bayard 
Taylor undertook the work at the suggestion of the well-known actor, 
Lawrence Barrett. 

As a result of correspondence carried on with Mrs. Taylor in 1911 
and i912, I was allowed to examine the manuscript. It was sent to 
Cambridge, and remained in the Treasure Room of the Harvard 
Library from May until December 1912. With the permission of 
Mrs. Taylor and of Mrs. Kiliani, her daughter, I published an article 
describing Taylor’s work (F. W. C. Lieder, “Bayard Taylor’s Adap- 
tation of Schiller’s Don Carlos” in The Journal of English and Ger- 
manic Philology, vol. XVI, no. 1, pp. 27-53). 

With Taylor’s manuscript there came also Lawrence Barrett’s out- 
line (in his own handwriting) giving his ideas of how Schiller’s play 
was to be adapted for the American stage — two large foolscap sheets 
written on both sides and a small sheet with only a few lines written 
on it. Barrett indicates how Don Carlos is to be made an actable play. 
Certain dramatic motives are to be more strongly emphasized — the 
suspicion cherished by Alba and Domingo against Carlos, the power 
of Alba and Domingo over Eboli, Eboli’s love for Carlos, Carlos’s love 
for the Queen, Carlos’s denunciation of Posa when the latter is sus- 
pected of treachery. Dialogues are in most cases to be shortened; the 
language is to be made stronger. The audience is to be reminded con- 
tinually that Posa is working for Carlos. The scene with the Inquisitor 
is to be omitted. In other words, the plot is to be simplified as much as 
possible. Don Carlos is to be a play, not a dramatic poem. 

Taylor’s manuscript adaptation of Schiller’s play comprises one 
hundred and twenty-five unruled sheets in Taylor’s own handwriting. 
The last sheet is signed “Bayard Taylor, Jan. 14, 1878, New York.” 
It will be remembered that in 1878 Taylor was appointed minister to 
Germany by President Hayes; he died in Berlin on December 19, 1878. 
His adaptation of Don Carlos, therefore, was practically his last piece 
of literary work. 

Taylor followed closely Barrett’s directions. In my article I have 
reprinted Barrett’s outline in full; several quotations in that outline 


60 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


indicate that he used Boylan’s translation as a basis. As for Taylor’s 
adaptation, I followed the following scheme: first, to show what scenes 
in Schiller’s play correspond to the scenes in Taylor’s adaptation; 
secondly, to quote important passages from Taylor’s adaptation; 
thirdly, to show how. Taylor translated those lines that have become 
familiar quotations from Don Carlos; fourthly, to sum up and empha- 
size the importance of Taylor’s attempt to make Schiller’s play actable 
on the American stage. : 

Taylor, by following Barrett’s directions, managed to cut down 
Schiller’s original by more than half (Schiller has 5370 lines, Taylor 
has only 2599). The play reads well and seems to be fitted for the 
American stage. Taylor made the ending particularly effective. 
Carlos is turned over to an officer, not to the Grand Inquisitor. Carlos, 
according to the stage directions, ‘‘suddenly shakes himself free from 
the grasp of the officers, and stands proudly erect, pointing at the 
King”’; then he speaks the closing lines of the play: 

I meant to live: I meant to help and bless 
My suffering people, — but the end has come. 
You are no more my father and my king: 
Your face says death — you are my murderer! 


Go, branded by a blacker crime than Cain’s, 
Till death shall scourge you into history! 


During the war, when Mrs. Taylor moved from New York to Par- 
thenkirchen in South Germany, she lost track of the manuscript. Let 
us hope that some day it may be recovered. 


91. JOHN Exrorp, Philip IT. A dramatic romance, London, 1880. 


Elford’s Preface begins: “‘ No legend of modern times has had as much 
attention paid to it as that of the loves and sorrows of Don Carlos, 
Prince of the Asturias, and Isabel of Valois, Philip II’s young and 
beautiful queen.” In spite of this broad statement, Elford refers only 
to Brantéme, Otway, Alfieri, and Schiller. He regrets that the story 
of the mighty wrong endured by Carlos and Isabel at the King’s hands 
has been kept in the background by Alfieri and Schiller, thus making 
the unhappy lovers stand out as much sinners as sinned against. 

Elford concludes his Preface as follows: 

In the following version of the legend I have tried to present the com- 
monly received incidents of the story — the betrothal of the young couple, 
its rupture by Philip, and the consequences thereof — in such a way that 


our sympathy for Carlos and Isabel in their trouble is, I hope, never felt to 
be a divided or imperfect one. 


The Don Carlos Theme 61 


The play covers two hundred and forty-two pages (there are 22 
pages of notes), is divided into five acts, and is written in a combina- 
tion of prose and blank verse. Carlos is turned over to the Inquisition. 
The last scenes give the sickening details, related by the hired mur- 
derers, of the torture and death of Carlos. 

There is a copy of the book in the Harvard University Library. 


92. Ern Wort. Roman von Georg Ebers, Stuttgart und Leipzig, 1883. 


The Preface, which is dated November 10, 1882, states that the author 
dedicates the book to Dr. Carl von Burkhardt. Many of the scenes 
of the novel are laid in the court of Philip II of Spain. Ebers, a trained 
historian, represents Don Carlos as he really was — a deformed and 
eccentric youth. At one point Ebers gives an account of a court ball, 
and has a young artist named Ulrich describe the dignitaries who were 
present. The description of Don Carlos (p. 196) is strikingly accurate: 
“Der verwachsene Thronfolger Don Karlos beunruhigte mit plumpen 
Scherzen eine Reihe von Hofdamen, welche die Facher vor das Gesicht 
hielten, und nicht wagten, den Sohn des Herrschers ihr Misfallen 
fiihlen zu lassen.” 


93. C. Martinez, Der Dimon des Siidens. Historischer Roman, Leip- 
zig, 1883. (In three volumes bound in one.) 


The title of the novel is doubtlessly suggested by Schiller. In line 
1758 of his Don Carlos, Schiller, in referring to King Philip II, talks of 
“dem berithmten Handelsmann in Siiden.”’ In his translation of the 
Introduction (Précis historique) of Mercier’s Portrait de Phillippe second 
(this translation was published in the Thalia, Heft 2 [1786], pp. 71- 
104) Schiller uses the expression “‘ Dieser Damon in Siiden.” 

The general plot of the novel is neatly outlined by Martinez in one 
of his paragraphs (I, 75): 

Die dritte Vermahlung Philipps II mit der Braut seines eigenen Sohnes 
Don Carlos hatte die treuen Niederlander gegen den grausamen Vater nicht 
besser gesinnt gemacht. Ihm galt es also selbst das ganze Lebensgliick des 
einzigen Sohnes nichts, wenn er seine Wiinsche befriedigen wollte! Man 
wusste, das Don Carlos seine Braut und jetzige Stiefmutter liebte und ver- 
ehrte, und dass er seit der Stunde, wo der Vater die Braut fiir sich nahm, 
den Kénig mit der ganzen Glut des Siidens hasste. 


The novel has the usual complications and motives that are found in 
St. Réal, Schiller, and their followers. Carlos dies as a result of im- 
prisonment.

95. DER Prinz Don Cartos. Die Grodsste Tat DES KAISERS 
Kari V. Zwei Dramen von Don Diego de Enciso aus dem Spani- 
schen in fiinfftissigen Iamben tibertragen von Adolf Schaeffer, Leip- 
zig, 1887. 

Schaeffer’s work may be regarded as one of the most important adap- 

tations of Enciso’s work. He used two editions of the Spanish text 

— that of 1689 and that of 1773 (both published at Valencia). The 

edition of 1689 had a happy ending, which Schaeffer regards as in- 

consistent with the aesthetic taste of the German public: Carlos is 
miraculously cured by S. Diego of Alcala from fever and from injuries 
sustained through a fall. For this happy ending Schaeffer substitutes 
the tragic ending of the edition of 1773: Carlos in a fever sees visions 
of Philip II with Queen Anna, of Philip III with Queen Margaret, and 
of Philip IV with his Queen; while he speculates about the future, the 

King enters, realizes that Carlos is mortally ill, has him put to bed, and 

soon afterwards is informed that the Prince has died. 

Eboli and Posa do not appear in the drama. Carlos is not in love 
with his stepmother. He is represented as a high-strung, impetuous, 
feverishly impatient youth. The various characters are best described 
by Schaeffer (p. 6): 

Wir finden in unserem Trauerspiel folgende Charaktere: Don Carlos, von 
einem verzogenen Kinde zu einem ziigellosen Menschen aufgewachsen; 
Philipp II, als wiirdigen aber pedantischen, staatsklugen, zihe an seiner 
Autoritat fest haltenden Herrscher; Herzog Alba als unerschrockenen, weder 
im Verkehr mit dem K6nig, noch mit dem Prinzen je seine Fassung verlieren- 
den Mann des Eisens; Cardinal Espinosa (Grossinquisitor) als Giinstling des 
K6nigs, den die Ungnade seines Herrn im letzten Aufzuge ganz zu Boden 
schmettert. — Von den Charakteren des Schiller’schen Stiicks hat nur der- 


The Don Carlos Theme 63 


jenige des Kénigs einige Aehnlichkeit mit dem Enciso’schen; Gegensiitze 
sind: Don Carlos, der ideale Liebes- und Freiheitsschwarmer; Herzog Alba, 
der Rankeschmied, der‘sowohl vor dem Konig, als vor dem Prinzen zittert; 
umgekehrt der Cardinal Espinosa, vor welchem sich der Konig demiithig 
beugt. 


Carlos has the fixed idea that his father’s courtiers are plotting 
against him, and are seeking to keep him from winning his father’s 
favor and from sharing in the government of the realm. He wishes to 
be sent to Flanders. He is in love with Violante (Alba’s niece), who in 
turn is in love with Fadrique (Alba’s nephew). The King finally de- 
cides that his son is incurably ill. Carlos falls into a raging fever and 
dies. 

The King remarks briefly: 


Der Fieber war der Anlass seines Todes. 


To this Ruy Gémez de Silva replies: 


Nicht minder aber die Unmissigkeit 

Der Lebensweise; Immer Schnee geniessen, 
In nassen Tiichern schlafen, kaum bekleidet 
Zu gehen, alles tiberhaupt zu thun, 

Was seinem Korper nicht zutraglich war. 


And the King summarizes the situation: 


Sein Naturell hat mehr als alles Andre 
Ihm Tod gebracht. (Act III, Scene xxv.) 

In the last scene (Act III, Scene xxvi) the King summons Fadrique 
and Violante, tells them to join hands, grants them an income of ten 
thousand ducats, gives Alba orders to proceed to Flanders, and asks 
Don Diego to try to relieve the general tension by saying cheerful 
things. At the close of the play all those on the stage shout: 

Und hier, mit einem Beifallruf, wenn dessen 


Der Dichter wiirdig ist, schliesst ab ‘‘Der Prinz 
Don Carlos’; habet Nachsicht mit den Mangeln! 


Although Schaeffer has taken over from the edition of 1773, as he 
says, “nur einen verschwindend kleinen Teil,” yet this small part 
(about 100 lines) contains the remarkably effective tragic ending. He 
defends his procedure (p. 9) as follows: 

Obgleich ich mir leider der Vermuthung nicht erwehren kann, dass diese 
Abweichung (trotz der einfachen Angabe unsres Dichters als Autor am 


Anfange des Drucks) von einem Ueberarbeiter herriihrt, habe ich dieselbe 
aus dem oben angegebenen Grunde doch adoptiert. 


64 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


Three years later, in his Geschichte des Spanischen Nationaldramas 
(Leipzig, 1890), Schaeffer ventures the guess that the tragic ending is 
the work of Cafiizares (see nos. 4 and 19). 

Schaeffer’s adaptation has long been out of print, and is not to be 
found in the important libraries of Leipzig. There is a copy in the 
British Museum and one in the Staatsbibliothek at Munich.

99. CHARLES Raymonb, Don Carlos. Drame en cing actes et onze 
tableaux Waprés Schiller. Représenté pour la premiére fois au 
Thédtre national de VOdéon le jeudi 15 octobre 1896, Paris, 1896. 


The drama is in prose and extends over two hundred and seven 
pages. The list of characters omits three names from Schiller’s list — 
Farnese, Medina Sidonia, and Mercado. Curiously enough, the first 
character on Raymond’s list is ““Le Grand Inquisiteur.”’ 

Raymond omits the following scenes found in Schiller: I, vii-ix; 
1, xxii; IL, vi, vii; LV, iv, vi, vill, x1, xiv—xvi, xxi—xxiv; V, vi, vil. In 
other words, Raymond omits twenty of Schiller’s sixty-nine scenes. 
Other scenes are reduced in length; frequently two or more of Schiller’s 
scenes are compressed by Raymond into one. 

A few other changes are of interest. Schiller’s “Zwei edle Hauser in 
Mirandola”’ become ‘‘Deux nobles families 4 Florence, les Strozzi et 
les Colonna’’; Schiller’s “Fernando” becomes “‘ Rezzo”’; ‘“‘ Mathilde” 
becomes ‘Bianca.’ The play ends (as in Schiller) with the King’s 
words: ‘‘Cardinal, j’ai accompli mon oeuvre, faites la votre.” 

There is a copy of Raymond’s adaptation in the British Museum. 


100. Francis MARION CRAWFORD, In the Palace of the King. A Love 
Story of Old Madrid, New York, 1900. 

The time at which the events of the novel take place is November, 
1574. The principal characters are King Philip II, Eboli, Medina 
Sidonia, Don John of Austria, Ruy Gomez, Pérez, Alva, Mendoza, and 
the latter’s daughter, Maria Dolores. Although Don Carlos and 
Elizabeth of Valois had died six years before, their fate is frequently 
alluded to. A few passages will illustrate this point. 


It was well known in the court that two or three years earlier, during the 
horrible intrigue that ended in the death of Don Carlos, the Princess of 


66 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


Eboli had done her best to bring Don John of Austria to her feet, and had 
failed notoriously, because he was already in love with Dolores. [Page 239.] 

A high voice pierced the low roar, keen and cutting as a knife, but no one 
knew whose it was, and Philip almost reeled as he heard the words, ‘“‘ Re- 
member Don Carlos! Don John of Austria is gone to join Don Carlos and 
Queen Isabel!” [Page 258.] 

They were quite sure that Don John of Austria had been murdered by the 
King’s orders, like Don Carlos and Queen Isabel and a fair number of other 
unfortunate persons. [Page 292.] 

If you will not write the order to release my father, I shall go out at once. 
In ten minutes there will be a revolution in the palace, and to-morrow all 
Spain will be on fire to avenge your brother. Spain has not forgotten Don 
Carlos yet! There are those alive who saw you give Queen Isabel the draught 
that killed her — with your own hand. [Page 320 — Dolores to the King.] 

Either the King had meant to kill Don John himself, or he had ordered 
Mendoza to do so. That much was clear to any one who had known the 
secret of Don Carlos’ death, and the dwarf had been one of the last who had 
talked with the unfortunate Prince before that dark tragedy. [Page 336.] 

He [the jester] knew her [Princess Eboli’s] secret, too, as he had formerly 
known how she had forged the letters that brought about the deaths of 
Don Carlos and of Queen Isabel. [Page 337.] 


Crawford’s novel is an excellent example of a treatment in which the 
spirit of Don Carlos influences the characters, even though the Prince 
himself is no longer living. 


tor. EMILE VERHAEREN, Philippe II, tragédie en trois actes, Paris, 
1goI. 

A later edition of the play (1909) has the description “Episode dra- 
matique en trois actes.’”’ The form is a mixture of prose and of Alexan- 
drine verse. The principal characters are Philip, Carlos, the Countess 
of Clermont, the confessor Bernardo, Don Juan, Don Francisco de 
Hoyos, the priest Hierénimo, the Count of Feria. The minor char- 
acters are soldiers and monks. All the acts take place in the rooms or 
the garden of the Escurial. 

Carlos meets his mistress, the Countess of Clermont, in the garden 
of the palace. Interesting it is to note that Carlos is represented as 
being in love, not with the Queen, but with her lady-in-waiting, Cler- 
mont. During the love-scene, Carlos expresses his hopes of escape 
from Spain and of a glorious career in Flanders — hopes in which later 
he is apparently supported by his friend Don Juan. 

The King’s suspicions are aroused by his father-confessor, Fray 
Bernardo. Although Carlos and Clermont protest against the accusa- 


The Don Carlos Theme 67 


tions, the King finally decides to follow his confessor’s advice. Carlos 
is condemned and is strangled by soldiers. The King enters the room 
just as Carlos is breathing his last, and he falls on his knees before the 
body of his son. 

The story, as evolved by Verhaeren, differs from that told by St. 
Réal; Carlos and the Queen are vot in love. At the same time, the 
story does not follow the views of the early Spanish dramatists and of 
modern historians; Carlos is not represented as being the victim of his 
own excesses. DuLong’s theory (II, 160-1620), that Verhaeren fol- 
lowed Gachard’s historical sketch (Don Carlos et Philippe ITI [Paris, 
1863]) has much in its favor. Verhaeren’s drama, one might say in 
conclusion, is one of the important recent treatments of the Don 
Carlos theme. 


102. Don Kartos. Trauerspiel in fiinf Akien von Schiller. Mit 
Benutzung der dlteren Ausgaben fiir die Auffiihrung eingerichtet 
von Eugen Kilian, Biihnenausgabe, Leipzig (1904). 


This stage version was performed for the first time at Karlsruhe on 
January 8, 1903. It is printed as number 4569 in the Reclam series. 

Kilian points out that a stage performance ought to last between 
three and a half and four hours. He objects to the methods hitherto 
employed in producing Schiller’s play; some directors have produced 
the play as it was finally published (the performance in that case last- 
ing at least six hours), others have presented the play in two parts on 
successive evenings, others have reduced the size of the play by omit- 
ting scenes that are not indispensable for a correct understanding of the 
drama. 

Kilian has retained every scene that helps the spectator in any way 
to grasp the threads of motivation (in this connection he calls the scene 
with the Grand Inquisitor “‘Das Juwel des Dramas’’), he has omitted 
all lines which in his opinion represent superfluous rhetoric, and he has 
inserted important lines from the “Thalia” version, from the first 
complete edition of 1787, and from the Hamburg manuscript version. 
Schiller’s drama, as now printed, contains five thousand three hundred 
and seventy lines; Kilian’s stage version (which can be played within 
four hours) contains about three thousand two hundred and twenty 
lines. The work of adapting Schiller’s drama for the stage has been 
skillfully done. 


68 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


103. Don Kartos, INFANT VON SPANIEN. Ein dramatisches Gedicht 
in fiinf Aufztigen von Friedrich von Schiller. Fir die deutsche 
Biihne bearbeitet und inszeniert von C. W. Schmidt, Halle a.d.S. 
(1904). 

The Preface of this version is dated Berlin, May, 1904. The author 

states that it is his purpose to give a version that can be acted in not 

more than four hours. His theory about the method of shortening the 
drama may be repeated here: 


Eine weitere Schwierigkeit bietet das Zusammenziehen der Szenen und 
auch hier war der Bearbeiter bemiiht, so weit wie méglich zu gehen, ohne der 
Wahrscheinlichkeit Eintrag zu tun. Alles Theatermidssige, besonders das 
gewaltsame Zusammenschieben von Szenen, die nicht zueinander geh6ren, 
wurde als Feind der Illusion streng vermieden und daraus erklart sich, das 
auch im Zusammenlegen der Szenen gewisse Grenzen innegehalten werden 
mussten. 


The five acts of the drama have twelve different settings. Seven 
sketches, showing the arrangement of the scenery and the disposition 
of the characters on the stage, are provided. Schiller’s stage directions 
are supplemented by helpful suggestions that are doubtless based on 
experience. Lines that are absolutely dispensable have been omitted; 
others, that can be recited at the discretion of the director or of the 
actor, are printed in small type. Thus of the one hundred and twenty- 
seven lines in Schiller’s first scene, Schmidt has omitted thirteen, he 
has printed thirty-nine in small type, and he has shifted six to the be- 
ginning of the second scene. All in all, Schmidt’s version is skillfully 
made. 


104. RICHARD MANSFIELD. An acting version of Schiller’s Don Carlos, 
1905. 

When Mansfield decided to perform one of Schiller’s plays to com- 

memorate the centennial of the poet’s death, his choice was finally nar- 

rowed down to Don Carlos and Wallenstein, and the former carried the 

day. 

Mansfield’s version is based upon R. D. Boylan’s English translation 
of Schiller’s drama. The preliminary performance was given at the 
Valentine Theatre, Toledo, Ohio, on October 27, 1905. It was a part 
of Mansfield’s repertoire during the season of 1gos—o6. (See Paul 
Wilstach, Richard Mansfield the Man and Actor [New York, 1908], 
PP- 435-437-) 


The Don Carlos Theme 69 


Before 1905 there had been only a single performance in English of 
any version of Schiller’s drama — that of Dunlap’s adaptation (no. 34) 
on May 6, 1799, at the Park Theatre in New York, and that one per- 
formance had been pronounced a failure. In other words, Mansfield 
is the only man who has performed more than once an English version 
of Schiller’s Don Carlos anywhere in the world since the original publi- 
cation of Schiller’s drama in 1787. 


105. Don Cartos. An Histortcat Pray. In Four Acts. Founded 
upon Fredrick von Schiller’s famous tragedy of the same name. By 
Espy Williams. Author of The Duke’s Jester, A Cavalier of France, 
The Man in Black, The Husband, Parrhasius. [190-.] 
The play is in a manuscript of fifty-five typewritten sheets with a 
paper cover on which is the imprint: “Selwyn & Co., Play Brokers, 
Knickerbocker Theatre Building, 1402 Broadway, New York.” The 
library card of the New York Public Library (where the manuscript is 
now preserved) gives the full name of the author as Espy William 
Hendricks Williams and his dates as 1852 to 1908. The card also gives 
the place and date of the origin of the manuscript as New York, [190-]. 
We may assume, then, that the play was written sometime between 
1900 and 1908. 

The scene of the play is laid in Madrid. The date is given as 1560 
(probably a misreading, on the part of the typist, for 1568). The 
author directs that the costumes be the same as ‘‘In the Palace of the 
King.” (F. Marion Crawford’s novel with this title was published in 
1900.) 

The characters are Philip, Carlos, Alva, Posa, Domingo, Lerma, 
Taxis, Feria, Medina, Mercado, Isabella, Eboli. The following char- 
acters employed by Schiller are omitted by Williams: Farnese, Clara 
Eugenia, Olivarez, Mondecar, Fuentes, the Grand Inquisitor, and the 
Prior. The Inquisition, in other words, plays no part in Williams’s 
drama. 

The action is swift and closely knit. Alva is represented as having 
planned the marriage between Philip and Isabella. Posa is in love with 
Eboli, who, in turn, is in love with Carlos. Eboli’s name is also Isabella, 
hence Carlos supposes that a note from her is one from the Queen. 
Upon information furnished by Eboli, the King and his courtiers come 
upon Carlos in the Queen’s apartments, and arrest him. In the last 
scene Eboli visits Carlos, and tells him of the illness of the Queen. 
After she leaves, Alva appears disguised in a monk’s garb. He carries 


70 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


a tray on which there is a meal, a pitcher of water, a decanter of wine, 
and two empty goblets. He puts poison into the decanter. Carlos asks 
for wine, and Alva fills a goblet. His silence, however, arouses the 
suspicions of Carlos, who immediately requests that Alva drink first. 
Finally Alva throws aside his disguise, and is about to kill Carlos. 
Carlos seizes Alva by the throat. The King and his court appear. 
Carlos grasps the second goblet and drains it. Eboli enters, and an- 
nounces the death of the Queen. Carlos dies as he utters the words: 
“Tsabella — yes — I — follow — thee.” 


SUMMARY 


In the preceding list of one hundred and five treatments of the Don 
Carlos theme we find many types of literary exposition — tragedy, 
comedy, novel, historical novelette, dramatic and dialogue poem, 
“‘Heldenbrief,” and opera. Thirty of the treatments are written in 
French, twenty-seven in German, seventeen in English (five of these 
are American), thirteen in Italian, nine in Spanish, eight in Dutch, and 
one in Portuguese. Four treatments are in manuscripts that have 
never been published — Becattini (no. 20), Tieck (no. 41), Bayard 
Taylor (no. 90), and Espy Williams (no. 105). Six others, though per- 
formed at least once, were apparently never published — Dunlap 
(no. 34), Liquet (no. 44), Raynouard (no. 50), Anonymous French 
(no. 58), Lambinon (no. 64), and Mansfield (no. 104). The literary 
treatments extend over about three centuries and a quarter, from the 
composition of the poem Diogenes in 1581 (only thirteen years after the 
death of Carlos and seventeen years before the death of Philip IT) to 
the appearance of the works by Crawford, Verhaeren, Kilian, Schmidt, 
Mansfield, and Espy Williams during the first decade of the twentieth 
century. 

Disregarding the comedies and burlesques (nos. 41, 71, 74, 86, 89) 
and the treatments that make only casual mention of Don Carlos with- 
out assigning to him an important réle, we find that the writers who 
were attracted by the story of the unfortunate prince dwell upon the 
conflict between father and son. This conflict manifests itself by the 
suspicious attitude of Philip toward Carlos and by the rebellious be- 
havior of Carlos toward Philip. In most of the treatments the cause 
of the conflict is found in the bitter rivalry of Philip and Carlos for the 
love of Elizabeth of Valois. The fiction that Philip robbed his son of a 
bride spread rapidly after the publication of St. Réal’s story in 1672. 


The Don Carlos Theme 71 


Of the six treatments that preceded St. Réal’s in point of time, two 
— the poem Diogenes (no. 1) and the biographical sketches by Bran- 
tdme (no. 3) — were utilized by St. Réal, and in them he could have 
found references to the love-triangle of Philip, Carlos, and Elizabeth. 
The other four treatments (nos. 2, 4, 5, and 6) were unknown to St. 
Réal. 

One of these four treatments, Le Patissier de Madrigal (no. 2), is dif- 
ferent from any of the later treatments; it simply tells of the appear- 
ance of a man who had once been condemned to death by his father, 
who had managed to escape with the help of courtiers, and who is sup- 
posed to be Don Carlos. Of the remaining three treatments that pre- 
ceded St. Réal’s — those by Enciso (no. 4), Calderén (no. 5), and Mon- 
talvan (no. 6) — the most important is Enciso’s. Enciso’s El Principe 
Don Carlos influenced not only Calderén and Montalvan; it influenced 
also three treatments that appeared later than St. Réal’s — Cafiizares 
(no. 19), Schaeffer (no. 95), and Herzog (no. 98). Cafiizares adopted 
an ending different from Enciso’s; Carlos, instead of being cured mirac- 
ulously from a fever, dies as the result of his wild excesses. The Enciso— 
Cafiizares version (followed later by Schaeffer) is the most important 
of those comparatively few treatments that make no mention of an 
affection on Carlos’s part for his father’s wife; it is, moreover, the ver- 
sion that corresponds most closely with historical facts. 

St. Réal, on the other hand, though he gives the reader the impres- 
sion of having used historical sources, emphasizes the rivalry of Philip 
and Carlos for the love of Elizabeth of Valois. This element in his 
Don Carlos, Nouvelle Historique (1672) appealed to later writers on the 
theme, especially to the important dramatists Otway (no. 10), Campi- 
stron (no. 13), Alfieri (no. 22), and Schiller (no. 27), and these drama- 
tists influenced numerous others. Thus fully nine tenths of the serious 
literary treatments of the theme are based, directly or indirectly, on 
the story as told by St. Réal. 

The story of Don Carlos is of fascinating and perennial interest. 
Levi, toward the end of his final chapter (in the 1924 edition), states 
that “la leggenda di Don Carlos é una della pit ricche e della pit sug- 
gestive della letteratura moderna”; Madame de Staél is equally en- 
thusiastic when she says (De I’ Allemagne, chap. 17), “le sujet de Don 
Carlos est un de plus dramatique que lhistoire puisse offrir”; and 
Campistron (in the Introduction to his drama Andronic) asserts boldly 
that Don Carlos ‘‘est le sujet le plus touchant et le plus singulier qui 
ait jamais été traité.” 


a2 Frederick W. C. Lieder 


One might well speculate whether the unhistorical and popular con- 
ception of Don Carlos, as found in St. Réal’s account and emphasized 
in so many later literary treatments, will ever be dispelled.
